L'emergenza in attacco, in casa Inter ha riaperto un vecchio dibattito. Serve o no una quinta punta? Visti gli infortuni in serie di tre attaccanti su quattro, forse in estate andavano fatti certi ragionamenti, sopratutto perché giocando con due punte fisse, un quinto attaccante sarebbe servito a Cristian Chivu.

"In base al rendimento di giocatori e squadra, oltre che ai risultati ottenuti, l’Inter deciderà pure se intervenire o meno nel mercato di gennaio per regalare a Chivu una quinta punta di spessore. Nonostante infatti le parole di Marotta, che ha specificato come tale operazione non rientri nei piani nerazzurri, la situazione potrebbe cambiare, eccome. Dopo quanto accaduto negli ultimi giorni, anzi nella prima parte di questa stagione, sono infatti in corso delle riflessioni sul tema. E qualora dovesse davvero presentarsi una di quelle occasioni decantate dal numero uno nerazzurro su un attaccante di un certo tipo, non si può escludere che l’Inter decida in questo caso di coglierla al volo", sottolinea Tuttosport.

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: How often do you buy a new car?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →


We have a bunch of new Hondas. None of them require the use of the touchscreen to adjust the climate controls. There are knobs and switches for all of that. There are physical controls for everything just like a car that doesn't have a touchscreen. The touchscreen is only used to configure certain options that only need to be set once. When I am driving, the touchscreen is used for Android Auto (Apple Car Play for Apple users). I don't touch the screen while driving.
How old are your cars? A quick search using Google AI resulted in the following when I asked what functions have touchscreens replaced:

• Climate and Comfort Controls. Temperature dials, fan speed buttons, heated seat switches, and even air vent direction controls moved inside touchscreen menus across many brands.
• Audio and Infotainment. Traditional volume knobs, tuning dials, and radio preset buttons were widely replaced by screen icons or flat capacitive touch sliders.
• Vehicle Settings and Core Functions. Drive mode selectors, headlight switches, windshield wiper controls, glovebox releases, and in some electric vehicles, turn signals and gear shifters migrated onto displays.

NBC Goes All In On Its Blue Bloods For Adults Replacement

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Network television has become increasingly cautious about committing to lengthy seasons, particularly when introducing new dramas. With streaming reshaping audience expectations and traditional viewing habits, securing additional episodes during a freshman season is an increasingly meaningful achievement. It also signals a network's confidence in a show's long-term potential.

Blue Bloods spent 14 seasons representing the consistency that broadcast networks strive to achieve. The CBS procedural combined family drama with traditional crime storytelling, developing a devoted audience throughout its run. Its conclusion left a noticeable gap in the television landscape, but its success demonstrated that audiences still appreciate character-driven procedurals.

NBC's Line of Fire has emerged as a promising successor, following the Hollingsworth family as they navigate their complicated relationships and careers in federal law enforcement. Starring Peter Krause and Hope Davis, the series brings a politically charged, morally complex approach to the family procedural, distinguishing itself from its CBS predecessor.

Now, NBC has demonstrated its confidence in Line of Fire by expanding its inaugural season from 13 to 18 episodes. The additional five episodes represent a significant endorsement of the freshman drama, suggesting the network recognizes the potential of a series that builds upon an established formula while introducing something distinctly its own.

NBC's decision to extend Line of Fire season 1 is supported by impressive ratings, establishing the freshman drama as one of the network's biggest successes of the 2026–2027 television season.

Originally ordered for 13 episodes, the series received an additional five, bringing its first season to 18 episodes. Notably, Line of Fire became the first new broadcast series of the season to receive an episode extension, demonstrating NBC's early confidence in its performance.

The numbers help explain the network's enthusiasm. Following its September 21 premiere, Line of Fire accumulated more than 14 million cross-platform viewers, including repeat broadcasts. Its second episode also experienced a 47% increase in the coveted 18 to 49 demographic compared to the premiere. These figures suggest that the show isn't simply benefiting from initial curiosity but is successfully encouraging viewers to return for additional episodes.

Beyond its impressive audience numbers, Line of Fire has helped strengthen NBC's Monday night programming. Airing after The Voice, the series considerably improved the network's 10 p.m. timeslot compared to the previous season's performance of Brilliant Minds. Its success across both traditional television and streaming also demonstrates its ability to reach viewers regardless of how they consume entertainment.

The additional episodes are particularly encouraging because Line of Fire has already established an ambitious overarching mystery that benefits from an extended season. Rather than rushing toward a resolution, the series now has more opportunities to develop its characters, explore the consequences of their discoveries, and build upon the conspiracy threatening the Hollingsworth family.

Although an expanded episode order doesn't guarantee a second season, it represents a substantial step toward establishing Line of Fire as a permanent fixture in NBC's programming lineup.

The similarities between Line of Fire and Blue Bloods are immediately apparent, particularly in their emphasis on multi-generational families working in law enforcement. Both series recognize that their characters' professional responsibilities become considerably more complicated when their personal relationships are involved. However, Line of Fire takes that premise further by making those familial connections a potential liability rather than simply a source of strength.

Throughout its 14 seasons, Blue Bloods centered on the Reagan family's commitment to justice, even when their differing perspectives created disagreements. Frank Reagan's position as NYPD Commissioner frequently complicated his relationships with his children, but the family remained largely united by their shared principles. Their weekly Sunday dinners reinforced that connection, reminding audiences that family came first regardless of their professional conflicts.

The Hollingsworth family operates under considerably different circumstances. Rather than working within different branches of the NYPD, they occupy positions across the FBI, Secret Service, U.S. Marshals, and Department of Justice. Their respective agencies often have competing priorities, creating opportunities for professional disagreements that extend beyond the family's personal struggles.

More importantly, the Hollingsworths aren't nearly as united as the Reagans. Mike and Jane's complicated relationship, their children's personal secrets, and the family's involvement in an increasingly dangerous conspiracy create divisions that threaten their ability to trust one another. Whereas the Reagans typically resolved their disagreements through communication and mutual respect, the Hollingsworths frequently conceal information from each other, even when those secrets could have serious consequences.

As CBS's 2026-2027 schedule goes deep into the fall season, the network finally confronts the painful truth about the Reagans in Blue Bloods.

The series also modernizes the traditional procedural format by incorporating an overarching mystery alongside its weekly investigations. Blue Bloods largely relied on self-contained cases, allowing audiences to enjoy individual episodes without following every previous installment. Line of Fire, meanwhile, uses its continuing conspiracy to give seemingly unrelated investigations greater significance while encouraging viewers to remain invested in the larger story.

Ultimately, Line of Fire doesn't need to replicate Blue Bloods to become its spiritual successor. By retaining the emotional foundation of a family-centered procedural while introducing greater moral ambiguity and serialized storytelling, NBC has created a drama capable of appealing to longtime Blue Bloods fans without feeling like a direct imitation. Its expanded episode order suggests that this updated approach is already paying off.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Roll-A-Rack debuting low-profile, manufactured-on-site solar racking

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Roll-A-Rack, a developer of patented, on-site-manufactured solar racking systems, will introduce SunChord, its new multi-purpose ground-mounted system, at RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas.

SunChord is a low-profile solar racking designed to use less land than conventional fixed-tilt racking or trackers. It is composed of two components: gutter channels and clamp brackets that attach to solar panels. SunChord assembles using what Roll-A-Rack has dubbed its “snap-slide-secure” system.

“The next generation of solar technology needs to do more than produce clean electricity. It needs to make better use of land, minimize environmental impacts and create value beyond the energy generated,” said Don Scipione, founder of Roll-A-Rack. “SunChord reflects our vision of harmonizing sun and earth, combining solar energy production with rainwater harvesting and soil conservation in a system that can unlock new opportunities for solar development on land where conventional approaches fall short.”

Roll-A-Rack claims an array using SunChord can capture 95% of rain that falls it using its racking channels to redirect the water. The company sees potential for the racking in agrivoltaic applications, sites with land constraints, on brownfields and where ground disturbance should be limited.

SunChord is assembled on-site using a roll-forming device similar to producing roof-edge gutters. SunChord was developed with support from the U.S. Department of Energy’s Integrated Energy Systems Office and the USDA National Institute of Food and Agriculture. The company has two issued U.S. patents, a patent application pending, wind-tunnel ratings and UL 2703 certification.

Roll-A-Rack is planning to supply SunChord for up to five pilot projects. Interested EPCs and developers can contact the company at [email protected].

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

PUT checked the project space, DELETE didn't: cross-tenant deletes in the CommCare HQ API (GHSA-hq38-78vf-f4cj)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CommCare HQ is the server side of CommCare, Dimagi's platform for building offline-first mobile apps for frontline workers. One instance hosts many project spaces, and the project space is the tenant boundary. Mobile workers are the field users of a project's apps. Lookup tables hold reference data that a project's apps and forms read.

A delete from another tenant takes away a project's field user or the data its forms depend on.

The API is built on django-tastypie. Each request names a project space in its URL, /a//api/... (the code calls a project space a domain). Authentication checks that the caller has the required permission in that project space. It says nothing about the object the request points at. That second check is left to each resource's own methods.

The mobile-worker resource, at commit 2ef6f1c from 2026-08-13:

The update handler loads the user and asserts it belongs to the URL's project space. The delete handler loads the user by ID alone and retires it.

The lookup-table resource has the same shape. Like its read handler, its update handler raises NotFound when the table's project space doesn't match the URL's. Its delete handler filters by ID only:

So a caller with permission to edit mobile workers through the API in project space X could send DELETE /a/X/api/v0.5/user// with the ID of a mobile worker in project space Y, and the request would pass authentication in X and retire the user in Y. The advisory lists the affected routes for users, lookup tables and rows across the v0.5, v0.6, v1 and v2 API paths.

The URL's project space authenticated the caller. The read and update handlers checked that the object belonged to it; the delete handlers didn't.

I didn't run a full CommCare HQ. On 2026-08-13 I ran a harness that drives django-tastypie 0.15.1, the version CommCare HQ pins, through its real request handling. Into it I copied CommCare HQ's obj_delete method from commit 2ef6f1c, and obj_update up to its project-space check, with the class names swapped for in-memory stand-ins. The harness seeded one synthetic mobile worker in domain_Y, and caller A made the request as a user of domain_X.

These are the delete lines of the harness's output, as recorded in my run notes (the harness's separator lines removed):

The delete from domain_X went through and marked the domain_Y user deleted. It returned 204, not the 202 in the handler, because tastypie returns 204 after a delete. A second harness ran the same delete for a lookup table and a lookup-table row, and both were removed.

The harness also tried the update as a control. On review, that step never reached CommCare HQ's code: the stand-in request object lacks a method tastypie calls on every PUT, so the PUT failed with a 500 before obj_update ran. It doesn't show the update check working. The check is in the source above, and the advisory describes only the deletes as affected.

Honest boundary: the harness ran tastypie's real request path, but with stand-ins for the request object, the database, authentication, CommCare HQ's base-resource dispatch check (which looks only at the caller's own project space), and retire() (a reduced copy that marks the user deleted). I didn't send HTTP requests to a running CommCare HQ. The impact on a live instance is as the advisory states it. The data was synthetic.

Fixed in commit 3570c0ff9c7, through PR #38029, merged on 2026-08-14. Each delete now looks the object up within the URL's project space:

Per the advisory, a foreign object ID now returns 404 instead of being deleted. The advisory asks self-hosted instances to deploy 3570c0ff9c7 or later. For anyone who can't upgrade right away, it suggests blocking the /a//api/ paths at the proxy, since per-user permission changes don't help when the attacker controls their own project space.

If you self-host CommCare HQ, make sure you've deployed 3570c0ff9c7 or later.

Reported by me; the advisory credits me as reporter. Comments and corrections welcome.

Santosh Kumar Puppala — AI/ML Platform Architect and security researcher (multiple CVEs; creator of Norviq). GitHub: @Santoshkumarpuppala

No more need to slap 'reddit' on the end of your Google searches as Reddit Reviews is here to amalgamate everyone's thoughts

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I'm no stranger to slapping "Reddit" on the end of a Google search about a product to see what the unwashed masses (myself included) think about it. Assuming we're not reading bot comments, it's a great way to get a feel for a product you've not tested yourself. That's traditionally involved trawling various threads yourself, but now 'Reddit Reviews' is launching, which will collect user opinions on products and use them to inform dedicated reviews for them on Reddit's site.

"Today, we are starting to test Reddit Reviews, a new experience that organizes product conversations from across Reddit into dedicated reviews and highlights the most important takeaways from across our communities. With Reddit Reviews, people can now more easily understand what a product is really like and feel confident deciding whether it’s right for them."

AI is involved, of course: "Reddit Reviews uses AI to both organize these conversations where people describe their product experiences, and to surface the nuance, context, and connection to the original conversation."

Once it rolls out to you, you'll be able to search Reddit for reviews or for categories of products, and you'll also be able to navigate to relevant categories and similar products from the review you're looking at. In each review, you'll be shown the ratio of positive, mixed, or negative conversations about the product, as well as peoples' likes and dislikes, and you'll be able to filter in/out what's most relevant to you.

There's another aspect to this, too—a monetary one: "Many reviews also include images, prices, and purchase links where supplied by brand and retailer catalogs."

Presumably that will be an affiliated link that gives Reddit some small percentage of of any money spent, though I can't say for sure. If so, this could be another crucial revenue stream for Reddit.

While Reddit Reviews is only rolling out to US iOS users over the coming weeks and will cover around 10,000 products, the company says, "Throughout 2027, we plan to add more products and categories to Reddit Reviews and expand beyond the US."

The PC Gamer Circuit Breaker newsletter breaks down the latest PC gaming tech, and finds you the best deals on the best products

Speaking of the elephant in the room, though—the possibility that people could be steered to Reddit for these amalgamated reviews rather than to sites like yours truly—I'm not too concerned. People already go to Reddit to check out user opinions on products; I do the same. I don't think collecting all those opinions up will change much in that regard, only make it a bit easier to do. It's a good move, I reckon.

Though how it pans out in practice is an open question. There's a chance, for instance, that it could open the door to review bombing. If enough people hate a company, for instance, they could band together to write badly about a product and then get its 'official' amalgamated Reddit review looking negative.

I'll be interested to see how (or if) Reddit deals with such things, as well as the opposite: companies using bots to speak highly of their own products.

Airdrop Scams, Decoded — Dusting, Drainer Links and Fake Claims

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 11 • Originally published at nexflowscan.com

Tokens you never asked for showing up in your wallet is not luck — it's a fishing lure cast at scale. The dust itself is harmless. What it invites you to do next is the attack.

Educational guide · reviewed August 2026 · not financial advice

Open almost any active wallet and you'll find them: tokens you never bought, NFTs you never minted, names like "5000USDT-voucher" or a famous project's ticker with a website baked into it. Sending them costs the scammer almost nothing, because on most chains a single transaction can spray a worthless token to thousands of addresses at once. The economics only work because a tiny fraction of recipients does the one thing the token exists to provoke — and that action, not the airdrop, is what empties wallets. When it has emptied one, the first hour after a wallet drain decides whether anything is salvageable.

This guide separates the three layers of the scheme — the dust, the destination, and the signature — so the next unexpected "reward" in your wallet reads as what it is: unsolicited advertising for a trap. Wallet software filters some of that noise and none of the consequence — is Phantom safe sets out what the app itself actually guards.

Paste its mint address before touching it — mass-dusted scam tokens tend to light up a scan immediately.

Legitimate projects airdrop for a reason you can articulate: rewarding past users, decentralizing governance, bootstrapping a network. Scammers airdrop for a different reason — an unsolicited token is the cheapest way to place a clickable message inside your wallet UI, past every spam filter that guards your inbox. The token's name is the ad ("Claim at ..."), its metadata carries the link, and your own curiosity does the delivery. Some campaigns add a pricing trick: the scam token trades against itself on a pool the scammer controls, so your wallet may display the dust as "worth" hundreds of dollars. That number exists to make ignoring it feel expensive.

The bait takes a few forms depending on the chain. Fungible dust — a few thousand units of a token named after a real protocol or a fake voucher. NFT spam — an image whose entire artwork is a URL and an instruction. Memo or metadata spam — transfer notes attached to tiny native-coin deposits, common on chains with cheap memo fields. All three share one property that matters more than anything else in this guide: receiving them changes nothing about your security. Tokens cannot execute code on arrival. No balance sitting in your wallet can move your other assets. The dust is inert until you interact with it or with the address it advertises.

The trap is participation, not possession. Every airdrop drain in the wild requires a step you take: visiting the printed URL, connecting a wallet, and approving something. Refuse that step and the entire kill chain dies in your token list, worth exactly nothing and threatening exactly nothing.

Follow the printed link (don't) and you land on a site built to feel official — cloned branding from a real project, a countdown, an eligibility checker that congratulates every address it's shown. The eligibility theater matters: being told your specific wallet "qualifies for 2,400 tokens" converts a stranger's website into your pending payout, and people protect payouts. The connect-wallet button works normally, because connecting is harmless and builds trust. The harm is queued behind the button labeled Claim, which does not claim anything — it asks your wallet for a signature or transaction whose true effect is written in the fine print your excitement is designed to skip.

Drainer kits are modular, and the request they serve depends on what your wallet holds. The table below is the field guide — the left column is what the site says, the middle is what the wallet prompt really does.

Two properties make these requests nastier than ordinary bad transactions. First, several are gasless signatures — nothing leaves your wallet at signing time, so the theft can execute hours later, disconnected from anything you remember doing. Second, an approval is durable: it survives until revoked, so a single careless click can sit dormant in your account like an unlocked door.

The correct response is deliberately boring. Hide the token using your wallet's spam controls, or simply leave it — it cannot hurt you by existing. Never open the URL in its name or metadata, never Google the token looking for a claim page (search ads are a major drainer distribution channel), and never try to sell or swap the dust: interacting with a scam token's own contract is precisely the engagement its designer wants, and some are built so any interaction reverts, wastes fees, or fires an approval request. Burning is likewise unnecessary — you'd be spending gas to tidy a threat that isn't one. If you did sign something on a claim site, treat it as an active incident: revoke the approval from a trusted revocation tool and move remaining assets to a clean wallet, in that order of urgency.

Legitimate distributions have a shape you can recognize. Eligibility is determined by a snapshot of past activity, announced through the project's long-established channels — never by a token materializing in your wallet with instructions. Genuine claim flows are hosted on the project's primary domain, linked consistently from every official surface, and the claim transaction interacts with a published, verifiable distributor contract; many teams simply send tokens directly with no claim step at all. And no honest airdrop, anywhere, asks for your seed phrase, an upfront "release fee," or an approval over unrelated assets. When an airdrop is real, you generally learn about it from the project. When the "airdrop" is how you learned the project exists, you already have your answer.

One more habit closes the loop: treat claim deadlines as a pressure gauge. Fraudulent flows lean hard on urgency — hours-long countdowns, "unclaimed allocations redistributed tonight" — because reflection is fatal to them. Established projects run claim windows measured in weeks or months precisely so nobody has to rush a signature. The more a flow insists you act immediately, the more certain you can be that waiting costs you nothing and protects everything.

A scan of the dropped token's address costs nothing and takes seconds — the opposite of a drained wallet.

No. Tokens are passive entries in a ledger; receiving one grants its creator no power over your other assets. Every real-world drain tied to airdrop scams required the victim to act — visit a link, connect, and sign something. The dust is the lure, and a lure in your tackle box catches nothing.

Neither. Selling means interacting with a contract written by a scammer, which can revert, waste gas, or prompt you for approvals; burning spends fees to remove something that poses no threat. Hide the token with your wallet's spam filter and move on.

Go to the project through a channel you already trusted before the announcement — its long-standing domain, its documented social accounts — and confirm the airdrop is described there with a claim flow on that same domain. Skip search results and ads entirely, and treat any flow demanding fees, seed phrases, or broad approvals as fake regardless of branding.

Assume an approval or standing signature now exists against your account. Immediately revoke recent approvals with a reputable revocation tool, then transfer remaining assets to a freshly created wallet, prioritizing whatever the signature touched. Speed matters more than certainty; some drainers cash out in minutes, others wait for a bigger balance.

Because wallet UIs often price tokens from whatever pool exists, and the scammer created a pool that quotes their own token at a fantasy price. The displayed value is self-reported by the attacker and cannot be realized — attempting to is the engagement the whole scheme is built around.

Turn Notes Into a Podcast with Two AI Voices

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 11 • Originally published at murmurtts.com

A notes-to-podcast workflow: reshape six lines of notes into a two-host script, assign two voices in Murmur Projects, generate, and export the episode.

Short answer: To turn notes into a podcast, rewrite the notes as a spoken dialogue with a name in front of every line, then render each name with a different voice. The writing step is yours: the app does not summarize notes or draft a script. What it does is take a finished two-host script, keep each speaker attached to a chosen voice, generate the lines locally on a Mac, and export the episode.

That division of labor matters. Most tools that advertise notes-to-podcast conversion are selling an AI writer plus a cloud voice. If you already know your subject, the writing is the part you should control. The slow, mechanical part is turning a good script into clean audio with consistent voices, and that is the part this workflow removes.

Notes are compressed for your eyes. They skip transitions, assume context, and lean on fragments. A listener has none of that context and cannot reread a sentence. Before any voice generation, convert each note into something a person could say out loud to someone else.

Keep the note file open beside the script while you work. You are not throwing the notes away, you are expanding them. The notes remain the fact check and the outline; the dialogue is the performance.

Here is a complete before-and-after from a fictional short episode about pre-ground coffee. The notes are six lines. The script is twelve turns, alternating between a Host and a Guest.

Notice what the notes do not contain: a greeting, a reason to keep listening, a definition of surface area, or a usable answer for the listener with the grinder question. The rewrite supplies those in the smallest number of turns that still sounds like a conversation.

Host: Welcome back. Today we're talking about pre-ground coffee, and why the bag you bought last month tastes flat. Guest: The short version is surface area. Grinding exposes more of the bean to air, so the aromatics leave faster. Host: So the grinder matters more than the brewer? Guest: For most people, yes. A consistent grind changes how water moves through the bed, and that changes what you taste. Host: Our notes also say grind size beats brew time. Is that fair? Guest: Mostly. If the grind is uneven, no timer will save the cup. Fix the grind first, then adjust time. Host: A listener asked whether a cheap burr grinder is enough. Guest: A burr grinder with a stable setting is a better start than a blade grinder. You do not need the most expensive one to hear a difference. Host: What is one change to try this week? Guest: Grind one dose coarse and one fine, brew both, and taste them side by side. Keep the one you would drink again. Host: That is the whole episode. Try the two-grind test and tell us which cup won. Guest: And use beans you already like, so the only variable is the grind.

Each note became a pair: the Host states or asks, the Guest answers. That is not padding. The second turn forces the script to explain the claim instead of asserting it, which is exactly what a listener needs. Six lines of notes became a dialogue of about 190 words, which is roughly 75 to 90 seconds of speech depending on pace.

A second voice gives the script permission to ask the obvious question, define a term, and signal a new section without saying "section two." It also creates a natural place for the listener's objection. When the Host asks whether a cheap grinder is enough, the listener hears their own question in the show.

The tradeoff is real. Two voices are harder to keep consistent across episodes, and a scripted exchange can sound stiff if every answer is the same length. A solo read is the better choice for dense reference material, a personal essay, or anything where a second character would be theater. If the notes are mostly a list of facts, one narrator is honest. If the notes contain a question you expect listeners to have, a second host earns its place.

Pick the format before you write a single line. Changing format halfway through means rewriting the script and reassigning speakers, which is more work than deciding first. For most notes-to-podcast projects, the two-host explainer is the fastest structure to write and the easiest to follow.

Murmur is a local voice studio for Apple Silicon and Intel Macs running macOS 15 or later. Projects is its multi-speaker workspace, and it is the part that matters here: a project holds a shared cast, one or more scripts, and a timeline. The steps below follow the current app.

Projects is a Pro feature. The free plan covers Kokoro and scripts up to 1,500 characters, which is enough to test a voice but not to produce an episode. The app can start a one-click 3-day Pro trial, and the full license is $49 one-time with a 7-day refund policy. If you only need a single narrator for short clips, the free plan may be all you need.

One honest limitation: the app does not write, summarize, or restructure your notes. If you want the tool to invent the dialogue, this is the wrong workflow. Bring a script you are willing to stand behind. The app's job starts when the writing stops.

Two similar voices are worse than one good voice. The listener loses track of who is speaking and the conversation turns into a blur. Audition both voices on the same sentence before you commit, and listen on a laptop speaker rather than headphones, because that is how a lot of people will hear the show.

If the two voices still sound alike after auditioning, change the model rather than stacking effects on the export. Pitch shifting a finished voice makes it sound processed. A different preset or a different model family is the cleaner fix.

Read the dialogue out loud once, all the way through. This catches most problems before they become audio: a sentence that cannot be said in one breath, a name with two possible pronunciations, a number that reads differently as a figure than as a word. Fix the script, not the take, when the problem is in the writing.

The podcast script example shows a different structure for a solo episode, and the multi-voice podcast workflow covers sponsor reads and section-by-section revision. Both are useful comparisons if your notes do not naturally split into a conversation.

The app exports the timeline mix as WAV or M4A. Use WAV when the episode will be edited further, normalized, or mixed with music. Use M4A when you want a smaller delivery file, and note that timeline markers become chapter markers in M4A. The export sheet can also write separate clip files, a text transcript, and a CSV of marker times.

The transcript is not an optional extra. The W3C's media guidance states that prerecorded audio-only content needs a transcript to meet WCAG Level A, and lists identifying speakers as an optional extra. Apple Podcasts generates transcripts automatically after publication and also accepts a provider-supplied VTT file, which can identify every speaker with each line. Murmur's own transcript export is plain text, so you would convert it to VTT yourself. If you export a plain text transcript from the app, keep it as the source for the published version and check names and numbers by hand.

The WAV, M4A, and MP3 export guide explains which file to keep as a master and which to send to a host. If your notes are study material rather than a show, the study notes to audio workflow is a closer fit than a two-host format.

That checklist takes a few minutes and prevents the most common failure: a polished voice track that says the wrong thing. The Projects workspace keeps the script, cast, and timeline together, so a correction is a block regeneration rather than a rebuild.

Can this workflow turn my notes into a podcast automatically?

No. Murmur does not summarize notes or write a script. You or an assistant you choose produce the two-host dialogue, and the app renders it with the voices you assign. Its value is the multi-speaker project, local generation, and export, not the writing.

How long should a notes-based episode be?

There is no correct length. A short explainer may need three minutes, while a teaching episode may need twenty. Let the notes decide: if a section does not change what the listener knows or does, cut it. Time the finished audio rather than estimating from the word count.

Do I need two different models for two voices?

No. A project uses one model, and each speaker is assigned a voice that model supports. Kokoro offers preset voices, and cloning-capable models can use saved reference profiles. Choose the model first, then confirm it has two voices you can tell apart.

Can I fix one bad line without regenerating the whole episode?

Yes. Generated audio sits on the timeline as clips. Select the clip for the line you want to change, edit the script text if needed, and use Regenerate Block. The rest of the episode stays as it was.

Should I tell listeners the voices are synthetic?

Disclosure rules vary by platform, sponsor, and audience, so check the requirements that apply to your show. A short line in the episode or show notes is usually enough. Never clone a voice without owning it or having written permission.

The extraction shooter-slasher game enters early access on Steam on 3rd December, and there's good news if you're eager to try it out before then.

Hunger just came off a very successful closed beta, which clearly didn't suffer by kicking off right alongside Modern Warfare 4's DMZ early access. Hunger is currently among Steam's most-wishlisted games, with over 700,000 wishlists, according to the developer.

The launch version includes three large maps, six playable characters, and five Mastery trees with more than 100 Masteries to unlock for your characters. Hunger has only been announced for PC so far, but will presumably arrive on consoles following its early access journey.

CosyVoice on Mac: CosyVoice 2 and 3 Setup Guide

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 11 • Originally published at murmurtts.com

Run CosyVoice 2 or 3 on an Apple Silicon Mac: what each version changed, the official install steps, the macOS GPU gaps, and when a shipped app fits better.

Short answer: CosyVoice is an open source text to speech family from researchers at Alibaba Group, built around a language model plus a flow matching decoder, with zero-shot voice cloning from a reference clip. Murmur does not run CosyVoice. On a Mac you run it yourself from the official repository, and the published install path assumes Linux with Python 3.10, so plan on CPU inference or community patches before you get Apple GPU acceleration.

That distinction matters because search results mix three different things: the research papers, the GitHub repository, and the hosted demos. The papers describe model behavior. The repository describes one install recipe. Neither one promises a supported macOS build. This guide sticks to what the project's own README, model cards, papers, and issue tracker say, and it marks the places where Mac users are on their own.

The project now lives under the QwenAudio/CosyVoice repository (the older FunAudioLLM links redirect there) and is licensed Apache-2.0. The architecture splits the job in two: a text to speech language model predicts supervised semantic speech tokens, then a conditional flow matching model turns those tokens into a Mel spectrogram for a given speaker, and a vocoder produces the waveform. The CosyVoice 2 paper describes a gradual semantic decoding procedure and says the design supports both streaming and non-streaming synthesis in one model.

The app ships a different catalog: Kokoro (bundled), Chatterbox Turbo, Chatterbox Multilingual, Qwen3-TTS Base, Voice Design, SparkTTS, Fish Audio S2 Pro, Breeze TTS 2, and OmniVoice. CosyVoice is not among them, and no setting in the app will make it appear. If you want CosyVoice behavior specifically, you need the Python project. If you want a Mac app that handles scripts, speakers, takes, and export without a terminal, compare the shipped models first in the local TTS model guide.

One more piece of small print: the README ends with a disclaimer that the content is for academic purposes and demonstrates technical capabilities. The repository license is still Apache-2.0, and both Hugging Face model cards list apache-2.0, but read the license and the card yourself before you build a commercial product on the weights.

The CosyVoice 3 paper says the language model was scaled from 0.5B to 1.5B parameters and the flow matching model from 100M to 300M, and that this larger capacity improved its multilingual benchmark. The checkpoint the README actually tells you to download is Fun-CosyVoice3-0.5B-2512, which is 0.5B. Both facts can be true at once: the paper reports experiments at two sizes, and the released default is the smaller one. If you see a claim that CosyVoice 3 on your Mac is a 1.5B model, check which weights that person actually loaded.

The version jump also changes the language story. The CosyVoice 2 paper reports training data in Chinese, English, Japanese, and Korean, and its limitations section says the model supports only a limited number of languages. The CosyVoice 3 README lists nine languages (Chinese, English, Japanese, Korean, German, Spanish, French, Italian, Russian) plus 18 or more Chinese dialects and accents, and both the README and the paper describe cross-lingual zero-shot cloning. The paper's data section counts 19 Chinese accents or dialects while the abstract says 18, so treat the dialect count as approximate and check the current card.

Zero-shot here means the model is not fine-tuned on your speaker. You supply a short reference recording and the matching text, and the model imitates the timbre, prosody, and style of that reference. In the CosyVoice 2 model card, the zero-shot call takes the text to synthesize, the prompt text, and the prompt wav path. A separate cross-lingual call takes the target text and the same prompt wav, and an instruct call takes a natural language instruction as a second argument after the text.

cosyvoice = AutoModel(model_dir='pretrained_models/CosyVoice2-0.5B') for i, j in enumerate(cosyvoice.inference_zero_shot('收到好友从远方寄来的生日礼物，那份意外的惊喜与深深的祝福让我心中充满了甜蜜的快乐，笑容如花儿般绽放。', '希望你以后能够做的比我还好呦。', './asset/zero_shot_prompt.wav')): torchaudio.save('zero_shot_{}.wav'.format(i), j['tts_speech'], cosyvoice.sample_rate)

The reference transcript is not decoration. CosyVoice 2's in-context learning mode uses prompt text and prompt speech tokens together, and the card's own examples pass the prompt text explicitly. The CosyVoice 3 card adds an instruction prefix such as "You are a helpful assistant." before the prompt text. If your reference clip is noisy, clipped, or in a different language from its transcript, the clone will reflect that.

Only clone voices you own or have written permission to use. A local model removes the upload step, not the consent requirement. For a broader workflow around reference recordings and takes, see the voice cloning guide.

The CosyVoice 3 README says the system supports text-in and audio-out streaming and "achieves latency as low as 150ms while maintaining high-quality audio output." That is the project's claim about its own streaming path. It is not a macOS benchmark, it does not say which hardware produced the figure, and it does not say how much text must arrive before the first audio chunk. Treat 150 ms as a best-case target to reproduce, not a number you should expect on an M1 laptop running CPU inference.

The CosyVoice 2 paper is more explicit about what the latency depends on. Its first-package latency is modeled as the chunk size M multiplied by the per-token time for the language model, the flow matching model, and the vocoder, summed together. Change the chunk size or the hardware and the number changes. The paper also reports that its streaming mode was nearly lossless compared with offline synthesis in typical tests, with a slight content consistency drop in hard cases. Those are paper results, not a measurement of your machine.

If you care about streaming behavior, measure it. The streaming latency measurement guide covers first-chunk timing and real-time factor on a Mac. A file-based editor is a different product category from a live streaming endpoint, so do not read this section as a claim about any desktop app.

The README's install section is short and Linux-oriented. These are its exact commands, minus the mirror flags the project adds for users in China:

git clone --recursive https://github.com/FunAudioLLM/CosyVoice.git cd CosyVoice git submodule update --init --recursive conda create -n cosyvoice -y python=3.10 conda activate cosyvoice pip install -r requirements.txt

The submodule step is not optional. The FAQ says a missing Matcha-TTS directory produces a ModuleNotFoundError, and that you should run git submodule update --init --recursive if third_party/Matcha-TTS is absent. The requirements file pins torch 2.3.1 and torchaudio 2.3.1, and it installs onnxruntime for darwin, so a macOS CPU install is plausible even though the README never walks through one.

The next step is model download. The README recommends the 3.0 checkpoint for better performance and shows both ModelScope and Hugging Face calls. For Hugging Face, the lines are:

from huggingface_hub import snapshot_download snapshot_download('FunAudioLLM/Fun-CosyVoice3-0.5B-2512', local_dir='pretrained_models/Fun-CosyVoice3-0.5B') snapshot_download('FunAudioLLM/CosyVoice2-0.5B', local_dir='pretrained_models/CosyVoice2-0.5B')

After that the README says to follow example.py, or to start the web UI with python3 webui.py --port 50000 --model_dir pretrained_models/CosyVoice-300M. The README also mentions an optional ttsfrd resource for better text normalization and says that if you skip it, wetext is used by default. Skip it on a Mac. The wheel it installs is named ttsfrd-0.4.2-cp310-cp310-linux_x86_64.whl, and issue #1011 shows that command failing on an M4 Mac mini.

Two more macOS details from the README: the sox troubleshooting commands are apt-get for Ubuntu and yum for CentOS, with no Homebrew line, and the deployment section builds a Docker image and runs it with --runtime=nvidia. The TensorRT-LLM acceleration path and the Docker examples are written for NVIDIA hardware. None of that blocks CPU inference, but none of it is a Mac install guide.

Issue #1011 is the clearest public record of the Mac experience. A user on an M4 Mac mini reports the CPU at 100 percent while the GPU sits idle, then quotes the device line in cosyvoice/cli/model.py: cuda if torch.cuda.is_available(), otherwise cpu. There is no MPS branch. Another commenter says support requires changing the code, and later comments show a patch that checks torch.backends.mps.is_available() before falling back.

Forcing MPS is not automatically a win. One commenter hits NotImplementedError for aten::unfold_backward on the MPS device, with the standard advice to set PYTORCH_ENABLE_MPS_FALLBACK=1 so that unsupported operations fall back to CPU. That operator is a gradient operation, so the thread mixes training and inference reports, and other commenters in it say MPS worked after upgrading to a newer PyTorch. Others report that newer PyTorch and newer macOS are needed, and that results vary by version. In issue #134 (July 2024, before CosyVoice 2 and 3), a project collaborator writes that an earlier MPS pull request was slower than CPU on Mac and that it was maybe not mature yet. These are user reports and maintainer comments, not a supported configuration.

The practical sequence is therefore: get CPU inference working first with the 0.5B checkpoint, confirm that a short line synthesizes and saves, then try an MPS patch on a copy of the repository and compare wall-clock time against CPU. Do not start by patching a fresh clone before you know the baseline works. And do not install torch nightly over the pinned 2.3.1 in the project environment without a separate environment to fall back to.

The honest summary: CosyVoice is worth the setup if you specifically need its multilingual and dialect behavior, or you want to study or modify the model. It is the wrong tool if you want a finished desktop editor, if you need Windows, or if you need a supported Apple GPU path today. The local studio versus DIY comparison covers that trade in more detail.

The repository LICENSE is Apache License 2.0, and the CosyVoice2-0.5B and Fun-CosyVoice3-0.5B-2512 model cards both list apache-2.0. Apache-2.0 permits commercial use and modification, with notice and attribution conditions. It does not settle the rights in your reference audio, and it does not override the README disclaimer. The open source model license checklist walks through the questions to ask before shipping.

Voice cloning adds a second layer. Use recordings you made, recordings you licensed, or recordings you have written permission to imitate. Do not clone a public figure or a private person to imply endorsement, and do not use a cloned voice for fraud or harassment. If a client supplies the reference, get the permission in writing and keep it with the project files.

No. The app ships Kokoro, Chatterbox Turbo, Chatterbox Multilingual, Qwen3-TTS Base, Voice Design, SparkTTS, Fish Audio S2 Pro, Breeze TTS 2, and OmniVoice. CosyVoice is not in that catalog. To use CosyVoice you run the official Python project yourself.

Can I run CosyVoice 3 on an Apple Silicon Mac?

You can try. The README's install steps are written for Linux with Python 3.10, and the code defaults to CUDA or CPU, so a Mac runs on CPU unless you patch it. Users in the project issue tracker report working CPU installs and community MPS patches, but the project does not document macOS as a supported target.

Is the 150 ms latency figure realistic on a Mac?

Treat it as the project's claim for its streaming path, not a Mac benchmark. The CosyVoice 2 paper shows that latency depends on chunk size and the per-token time of the language model, flow matching model, and vocoder. Measure first-chunk time on your own hardware before designing around a number.

Which CosyVoice checkpoint should I download first?

Start with CosyVoice2-0.5B if you want the longest-documented path, since its card has zero-shot, cross-lingual, and instruct examples, but note that the README itself recommends Fun-CosyVoice3-0.5B for better performance and that card has the same kinds of examples. The README recommends Fun-CosyVoice3-0.5B for better performance and lists nine languages, but its card's examples use the newer instruction prefix. Either way, confirm the local folder name matches the model_dir you pass.

The repository and both model cards state Apache-2.0, which allows commercial use subject to its notice and attribution terms. The README also carries an academic-purposes disclaimer, so read the license, the card, and the disclaimer together, and confirm that you have rights to the reference voice. This is not legal advice.

Sudokoo SK700V review

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An outstanding air tower, with stellar performance and a super-easy install process, complete with its own display. The only downsides? Intel support is extra, and it's strictly a "bring your own thermal paste" event.

PC Gamer's got your back Our experienced team dedicates many hours to every review, to really get to the heart of what matters most to you. Find out more about how we evaluate games and hardware.

Where do I even begin with this thing? I'm so conflicted on the SK700V. It's such a unique problem, simultaneously excellent, and yet surprisingly frustrating to talk about. I'll start with the positive. It's exceptional. For the price, the display, the cooling, the installation process, it's next level, and I've never seen anything like it. It's honestly impressive. Without a doubt, the best air cooler I've tested in years.

But, even with that glowing accolade there, boy does it irritate me.

Let's talk features first, though, before I start to wail on the minor inconveniences that have upset my tech media brain, because the SK700V has one pretty nifty detail straddling its heatpipes, and that's namely, a full-fat display gracefully sitting atop that shiny tower.

Yep, it's a screen we've finally got them folks, air towers with screens on them. And if Computex is anything to go by this year, we're going to be seeing a lot more screens on things. Graphics cards, CPU towers, heck, I'd be surprised if we don't see them on DRAM soon. That'll bring the price down, I'm sure of it.

✅ You want an affordable, compatible CPU tower with a display: Compatible with practically all RAM kits imaginable, with exceptional performance, and a display as standard, all for sub $100. Nice.

✅ You're running hotter chips: It's solid, but don't expect it to cool your overclocked Core i9 14900K, or AMD Ryzen 9 9950X3D. If that's the case you'll still want a beefy AIO behind them.

AM5, AM4, (LGA1851, LGA1700, LGA1200, LGA115X, with mounting kit)

It's not your full-fat OLED display, or anything fancy, but rather a simple digital-clock style system that actively reports on CPU frequency, temp, load, and power draw all on the fly. There's not much in the way of customisation in the software, but to be honest, there doesn't really need to be.

Sudokoo's generally quite an interesting company too. It's relatively new, founded in 2024, and is working in collaboration with a Taiwanese OEM with designs that remind me a lot of DeepCool's.

The SK700V is remarkably affordable for a licensed design. Sudokoo's pitching the SK700V at around $90 in the US and £80 in the UK, which is remarkably affordable given what you're getting here.

The PC Gamer Circuit Breaker newsletter breaks down the latest PC gaming tech, and finds you the best deals on the best products

I'm going to cover the irritating bits now, actually, because I want to get them out of the way, as genuinely there's a lot of good to talk about here.

Unboxing the SK700V is a real experience. The packaging is lush, pink and yellow. You open it up, and inside, in my case, there are two kits, an included Intel mounting bracket kit (which, annoyingly, Sudokoo sell separately), and a severe lack of thermal paste (although bizarrely, you do get some grease cleaner).

I don't understand why these two things are not included by default. I know that's a weird hill to die on, but there's literally zero reason why they shouldn't be included. This isn't some specially designed Noctua NH-L9i where the mounting is built directly into the low-profile frame.

This is a standard run-of-the-mill air-tower (albeit a very slim one), so why that Intel bracket isn't included is beyond me. Intel isn't dead, come on, guys. Oh, and the thermal paste too. Please, we're talking cents here, pennies, sweet naff all, include a tiny tube.

Installing it, though, was perhaps the most seamless experience I've ever had. Get your brackets set up, then take your tower, and slide the fan up and out of its rails (yes, rails). Underneath, you'll see some contact points for the display, and a hole for the included long screw driver on the back side. The front screw mount is easily reachable by any standard model. Then, once you're mounted, just slide the fan back in, clicking it into place, plug in the cables, and you're good to go. No funky, annoying metal clips, no awkward mounts, it's just brilliant. It's also remarkably compact; even with the fan in place, I could easily install memory after, no problem.

✅ You want an affordable, compatible CPU tower with a display: Compatible with practically all RAM kits imaginable, with exceptional performance, and a display as standard, all for sub $100. Nice.

❌ You're running hotter chips: It's solid, but don't expect it to cool your overclocked Core i9 14900K, or AMD Ryzen 9 9950X3D. If that's the case you'll still want a beefy AIO behind them.

The software, though, was a challenge on my machine. For whatever reason, Chrome, Brave, and Edge all flagged it as untrusted, and no matter what I tried with Windows, I could not for the life of me get it to download (this is less a problem with Sudokoo, more Microsoft, being, eh, well, Microsoft). In the end, I had to download it on a Linux machine, chuck it on a USB and bring it across that way. The software's fairly barebones, but it gives you all the system stats you need, and hooks up nicely with the SK700V as well.

As for performance. Stellar. Genuinely Stellar. I tested this at an ambient temp of around 26 to 27 degrees C, and it beat almost every other single tower cooler in our testing stack. In Baldur's Gate 3, it beat almost every air tower it went up against, bar Thermalright's Peerless Assassin 120 SE, but demolished everything in Metro Exodus, was pretty good in X264, beaten only by the Thermalright and the Arctic Freezer 36, not really winning any accolades, but monstered everything in Cinebench R23. A true testament to the design, given it was operating in ambient conditions four to five degrees higher than elsewhere.

The SK700V is, for a first attempt (and I know, yes, it's technically a licensed design), remarkable. At this price point, delivering this kind of performance, it's just brilliant to see. Particularly when the rest of the industry is on such a price hike at the moment, is it perfect? No. But if you're after an affordable air tower that looks good, and delivers while it does it, well, there are few I'd recommend more.

3. Best dual-towerThermalright Peerless Assassin 120 SE

An outstanding air tower, with stellar performance and a super-easy install process, complete with its own display. The only downsides? Intel support is extra, and it's strictly a "bring your own thermal paste" event.

Your Brain Keeps the Code It Writes. Not the Code It Pastes.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Your brain remembers what it makes. It barely notices what it reads.

That's not a productivity quote. It's one of the most reliable findings in memory research, and it has a name: the generation effect. If you write code for a living, and lately you've been letting an assistant write a lot of it, it's worth knowing about.

In 1978, Norman Slamecka and Peter Graf at the University of Toronto ran a set of experiments that sound almost too simple to matter. Half the time, people read a word pair in full. The other half, they got the first word and a fragment of the second, and had to fill in the rest. Think "hot" and "c___", where you supply "cold".

Same words. Same time on the page. The only difference was who produced the answer.

People remembered the words they produced better. Every time. Across five experiments, on recognition tests, free recall, cued recall and even confidence ratings, the "generate" condition beat the "read" condition. The authors noted the effect showed up at high levels of reliability "even in experiments employing only 12 subjects." You can read the original paper here.

They also admitted they didn't fully know why. "It is concluded that the generation effect is real and that it poses an interesting interpretative problem."

A single lab finding can be a fluke. This one wasn't.

In 2007, Sharon Bertsch, Michael McDaniel and colleagues pooled the research into a meta-analysis in Memory & Cognition: 86 studies, 445 effect sizes, more than 17,000 participants. The average benefit of generating over reading was about 0.40 standard deviations, or in their words, "almost half a standard deviation." That's a solid, ordinary-sized effect. Not magic. Not nothing.

A larger review in 2020, led by Matthew McCurdy at the University of Illinois Chicago, went through 310 experiments and found the effect held on every type of memory test they looked at. It also found something useful. The less constrained the generation, the bigger the payoff for recall. Filling in one obvious letter helps a little. Building the answer with more of your own thinking helps more.

There's one catch in the 2007 data that I find the most telling. When the material was nonsense, like made-up nonwords, the generation effect nearly vanished (an effect size of 0.05). Generating only helps when there's something already in your head to connect the new thing to. Your brain isn't rewarding effort for its own sake. It's rewarding you for wiring the new fact into what you already know.

Here's where it gets uncomfortable for developers.

Early this year, Anthropic published a randomized trial (full paper on arXiv) with 52 mostly junior engineers. All of them used Python weekly. None of them knew Trio, an async library. They built two small features with it, then took a quiz on the concepts they'd just used.

One group had an AI assistant in the sidebar that could produce correct code on request. The other group coded by hand.

The AI group averaged 50% on the quiz. The hand-coding group averaged 67%. The time saved by the AI group wasn't statistically significant. The biggest gap was on debugging questions, which is the exact skill you need when the generated code is wrong.

A field experiment in a Turkish high school, published in PNAS in 2025 by Hamsa Bastani and colleagues, found the same shape with nearly a thousand math students. A plain ChatGPT-style tutor boosted practice scores by 48%. When the AI was taken away for the exam, those students scored 17% lower than students who never had it. The researchers saw students using it as a "crutch," asking for answers and copying them.

Neither study is a generation-effect experiment in the strict sense. Different tasks, different tests. But they rhyme with it. When something else produces the answer, you end up in the "read" condition, even if your name is on the commit.

The Anthropic paper has the hopeful part. Using AI didn't automatically mean a low score. The people who did well asked follow-up questions, requested explanations, or asked conceptual questions while writing the code themselves. The ones who handed over the whole task got some speed and lost the learning.

Same in the PNAS study. A version of the tutor with guardrails, which nudged students toward working things out, mostly removed the harm.

So the tool isn't the problem. Who does the generating is.

You don't need a new system. You need small moments where your brain produces the answer before it sees one.

Guess the code first. Before you ask an assistant for a function, write the signature and a rough version, even a wrong one. Then compare. You've turned a read into a generate.

Retype, don't paste, the first time. When you're learning a new API, type the example from the docs by hand once. It's slower. That's the point.

Explain the diff in your own words. After accepting a suggestion, write one line in the commit message about why it works. Not what it does. Why.

Ask the AI to quiz you. Instead of "write this," try "give me a hint, not the answer." It's the guardrail version of the tutor, applied by you.

Close the tab and rebuild it. After reading a good explainer on, say, structured concurrency, close it and write three sentences from memory. Then check what you missed.

Every one of these is just the 1978 experiment again. A word with a gap, and you fill it in.

I build a small app called Recl, mostly because I kept saving good explainers and never thinking about them again. It turns what you save, like a talk, an article or a thread, into a short summary and then brings it back later as a question you have to answer yourself. That "answer it yourself" part is the generation effect doing the work, not the summary. You can see some example saves in the public library.

But you can get most of the benefit with a notebook and a little discomfort. Next time an answer appears instantly, try making it first.

I'm Asutosh. I write about how memory and learning work, and I build Recl, a tool for remembering what you watch and read.

Pergram examines the influence of newly elected lawmakers, the role of House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., and Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., and the potential impact of leadership decisions on the Democratic Party's agenda in the next Congress.

Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., says he will not support Sen. Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., to serve as Senate majority leader once Democrats take control over the chamber next year.

Sanders was complimentary of Schumer, but said at a news conference on Tuesday that he believes "the time is now" for change in Democratic leadership.

"I personally have known Chuck Schumer for over 30 years, and I like Chuck. [There’s] nobody who works harder than Chuck Schumer. But in my view, the time is now for change, and I will not be supporting Chuck to be re-elected as the Democratic leader," Sanders said at a news conference.

‘Train to Busan’ Director Yeon Sang-ho on AI Thriller ‘Paradise Lost’: ‘Something as Mysterious and Unknowable to Us as a Black Hole’

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After taking 'Colony' to Cannes earlier this year, the Korean filmmaker discusses AI, grief and family, and why a tight budget and a short shoot sharpened his latest film.

In Musan City, a decaying small town that exists only on screen, a grieving mother puts on a pair of AR glasses and spends time with her 10-year-old son. He has been gone for nine years, one of the children on a camp bus that vanished. Then the real boy comes home, grown up and a stranger, and the comfort the glasses offered starts to turn into something far more frightening.

That is the setup of “Paradise Lost,” the new psychological thriller from Yeon Sang-ho, the director of “Train to Busan” and the series “Hellbound.” It sits at the opposite end of the scale from “Colony,” which Yeon premiered in the Midnight Screenings section at Cannes earlier this year. “Paradise Lost” went on to Toronto’s Special Presentations section, where Yeon had also taken last year’s micro-budget “The Ugly,” and then screened as a Gala Presentation in Busan. Screenings sold out at both festivals. Related Stories Tom Hanks Returning to Broadway After 14 Years for New Play 'This World of Tomorrow' 'You've Got Mail' Reboot in the Works at Warner Bros.

Kim Hyun-joo, who worked with Yeon on both seasons of “Hellbound” and on “Jung_E,” plays the mother, Ryu So-young. Bae Hyeon-seong (“Hospital Playlist”) takes his first big-screen lead as Sun-woo, the son who returns. Yeon wrote the screenplay with Ryu Yong-jae, drawing on an original idea of theirs that author Oh Seong-eun turned into the novel “Black Inferno.” Wowpoint produced, and CJ ENM, which presents the film with Wowpoint, handles distribution. The film has sold to 118 countries, with Well Go USA handling North America, where it released Nov. 6.

“On one level, AI is something mechanical or technological. But when you look more closely, we can see what is beneath it. I think it is ultimately what we share as human knowledge and also collective intelligence,” Yeon tells Variety.

As he argued around “Colony,” that pooled knowledge is now beginning to threaten our individuality, he says, the very qualities that make each of us human. “The relationship between a mother and her son is perhaps one of the most deeply personal and intimate relationships we can have,” Yeon continues. “We can never truly know what lies in the depth of another person’s psyche.”

The film borrows its title from Milton’s epic poem. “Just like the story where the first of mankind are expelled from paradise, I thought that the loss of paradise is precisely where the human identity begins,” Yeon said in the film’s production notes.

He has used AI as a storytelling device before. In 2023’s “Jung_E,” an AI recreation allowed a daughter to free her mother from an idealized, self-sacrificing image, something he says would not be possible in the real world. “With ‘Paradise Lost,’ I wanted to take a much more grounded approach, using AI as a point of contrast, I would say, to explore something that is uniquely human,” he says.

AI may be built on humanity’s shared patterns, he adds, but it can also behave in very unpredictable ways, beyond our ability to fully control or comprehend. “In that sense, I think AI itself represents an uncharted territory. Something as mysterious and unknowable to us as a black hole.”

Where “The Ugly” followed a son digging into his father’s buried past, “Paradise Lost” swaps in a mother and son.

“I think the family is the very first form of society that we encounter as human beings,” Yeon says. “And because the family is our first experience of society, it plays a fundamental role in shaping much of our sense of self and also our personal identity.”

“The Ugly” was shot for KRW200 million ($150,000). “Paradise Lost” was also made lean, for KRW500 million ($374,000) over just 14 shooting sessions. Yeon leaned on Kim, whom he knew understood his directing style extremely well after several projects together. The schedule meant few takes and limited locations.

“But when you think about it, many of the most legendary films in Asian cinema were made under similar constraints,” Yeon says. “In a way I found that those limitations allowed me, and allowed us, to explore the material more creatively, and they also demanded an extraordinary level of focus and concentration from everyone involved.”

Korea’s theatrical industry is going through a major transformation, Yeon says, and some feel the market has shrunk considerably. “But I don’t necessarily see that as an obstacle to making good films. A smaller industry doesn’t automatically mean fewer great films will be made,” he says. Making “Colony” and “Paradise Lost” in the same year gave him firsthand experience of two radically different scales, which he hopes will help him lead productions of any size with greater confidence.

International sales covered the film’s net production budget ahead of release. Among Korean distributors, CJ ENM is one of the most active in working with overseas partners, Yeon says, and that experience counts. “Whether a Korean film is a box office success or not, whether it is a mainstream commercial production or something with a more niche sensibility, I believe there are audiences somewhere in the world who will appreciate it,” he says.

Making a film available everywhere is not enough on its own, he adds. “Global streaming platforms already release films simultaneously across multiple territories, using algorithms to recommend content based on individual preferences. But I don’t think we have necessarily established just how effective that approach is at helping films find their audiences.”

Yeon has two projects in post-production. The first is a Japanese-language Netflix series, based on the manga “Parasyte: Tamiya,” on which he serves as screenwriter and showrunner. It moves one of the original’s villains, Tamiya Ryoko, to the center of the story. “What I found particularly fascinating is the way Tamiya, who is a parasite rather than a human being, gradually tries to understand humanity,” he says.

The second is “Devotion,” a horror feature due next year. It grew out of the way people now put versions of their identities on display and hide behind the personas they build. Its Korean title, “Yeto,” is a Buddhist term for an imperfect world marked by suffering, the opposite of the realm of ultimate bliss.

For a filmmaker who thinks being human starts with the loss of paradise, it is a fitting next stop.

Maybelline updates ‘Maybe’ tagline as beauty perceptions evolve

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Miley Cyrus is leading a new global brand platform promoting changes meant to be more inclusive and empowering for women.

Maybelline is reworking one of its signature brand assets as it tries to adapt to an evolving landscape for beauty and cosmetics. The L'Oréal-owned marketer’s “Maybe she’s born with it” tagline became a fixture of its advertising beginning in 1991, but consumers in 2026 are applying makeup for different reasons.

The company has attempted to modernize “Maybe” in the past. It was at one point dropped for the largely forgotten “Make It Happen” but eventually brought back. Maybelline revamped the “Maybe” tagline for the TikTok era two years ago. The latest overhaul appears more substantial, changing the underlying meaning of the copy, and is described in the release as permanent.

Over half of surveyed global respondents today see beauty as a way to build confidence compared to 26% who see it as a means of looking more attractive to other people or appearing younger for longer, per McKinsey findings cited by Maybelline. The suggestion is that makeup can be something less superficial and more empowering and inclusive.

Tweaks to the marketer’s tagline try to recognize these trends by adjusting the phrasing to sound less like a question being asked about a single woman’s look. Instead, the new “Maybe” is positioned as a declaration of the possibilities all women can achieve by leveraging makeup as a form of self-expression.

“With 'Maybe.', we're building on our legacy and inviting women everywhere, at every stage of life, to explore the possibilities within them,” said Maybelline Global Brand President Sandrine Jolly in a statement.

Maybelline enlisted Cyrus, an ambassador who’s previously voiced the “Maybe” tagline, to lead its new “The Beauty of Infinite Possibility” platform. Cyrus was selected for the effort because her own career has entailed a constantly shifting identity, moving from child star to hard-partying club song purveyor and then back to her country roots.

Cyrus released her latest album, “Bass Persuades,” in September, which could draw additional interest to the Maybelline partnership. “Bass Persuades” sees Cyrus again dabbling in the dance pop and electronic music genres.

Products featured in the campaign include Maybelline’s Serum Lipstick, Lash Sensational Sky High Mascara and Instant Eraser Concealer. Maybelline will partner with additional women in the worlds of music and elsewhere to share their stories of using makeup as a form of expression and reinvention.

Consumer products, the division of L'Oréal that Maybelline sits under, grew 4.3% on a like-for-like basis in Q3, lagging other segments like dermatological beauty and professional products.

Telegram Doesn’t Need to Convert Crypto Users: 1M Wallets in 48 Hours Reveal Its Massive Distribution Advantage

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Telegram is gaining crypto users without having to convince them to download another app. More than 1 million self-custodial wallets appeared on the messaging platform within 48 hours. Founder Pavel Durov says the growth reflects a simple feature: wallets activate automatically when users receive Grams. The milestone shows Telegram’s biggest advantage in crypto: distribution.

Unlike traditional crypto wallets, Telegram’s native wallet operates inside an application millions already use daily. Crypto traders, project founders, airdrop hunters, and blockchain communities have long relied on Telegram. That existing audience gives its wallet a distribution advantage that standalone crypto applications must spend years building.

The latest growth also raises an interesting question about blockchain adoption. Does crypto really need another wave of new users, or does it need easier access for people already participating? Telegram could provide exactly that bridge, provided wallet registrations translate into meaningful economic activity.

Durov reported more than 1million new self-custodial wallets over 48 hours in an X post. The dashboard accompanying his announcement showed more than 1 million registrations across the period.

1M+ self-custodial Gram wallets activated on Telegram in just 48 hours. $GRAM is up 10% in 24h, the #1 gainer in the CMC top 100. pic.twitter.com/VGVTT5AZQk

According to Durov, users do not need to manually create a wallet before receiving crypto. Their wallets activate when someone sends them Grams.

This creates a potentially powerful viral distribution mechanism. One user sends tokens to another, introducing the recipient to an on-chain wallet. The recipient can then hold or transfer assets without leaving Telegram.

This approach removes several familiar barriers to crypto adoption. New users do not necessarily need to understand seed phrases, download separate applications, or navigate an exchange. Telegram already provides the social environment where these transactions can begin.

The platform also has more than one billion monthly users, giving its crypto ambitions an enormous potential audience. Even a small conversion rate could produce substantial wallet growth.

The latest wallet milestone is not the only sign of expanding activity across The Open Network (TON), the blockchain powering Telegram’s wallet. TON averaged 4.06 million daily transactions during October. The network also added approximately 2.73 million wallets throughout the month, its strongest monthly addition since October 2024.

Stablecoin activity offers another indication of growing utility. TON averaged 37,693 daily stablecoin users in October, representing a 23.44% increase from September.

The stablecoin figures are particularly interesting because they point toward activity beyond token speculation. Payments and transfers could give Telegram crypto a more practical role in everyday digital interactions.

TON’s stablecoin supply stood at $585 million today. Its decentralized finance total value locked, however, remained comparatively modest at approximately $44 million.

Payments, stablecoin transfers, digital collectibles, and Telegram’s mini-app ecosystem could provide reasons to return. These services could gradually make blockchain transactions feel like another ordinary messaging feature.
