Brazil Shows the Danger of Digital Chokepoints

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Brazil Shows the Danger of Digital Chokepoints

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

When Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva gave his victory speech in late October, he took one hand and held it aloft in tribute to “our general,” as his supporters cheered the “warrior of the Brazilian people.” The title was fittingly bestowed on André Janones, the 42-year-old congressman who vigorously mobilized almost 11 million voters as part of Lula’s “digital army,” marking the first time since Brazil’s return to democracy four decades ago that the runner-up surged ahead in the second round of a presidential election. It was a remarkable turnaround that will to be studied by election watchers for years to come.

In one of the most consequential elections in Brazilian history, some three-quarters of the population were getting their news from social media, and Meta—the owner of the most prominent platforms—was supportive of Sen. Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of the now-imprisoned former president. Lula’s campaign was allegedly thwarted online on Meta’s platforms. United Nations experts described as interruptions of “one side’s” social media accounts, curtailed audience engagement, and choked user feeds.

When Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva gave his victory speech in late October, he took one hand and held it aloft in tribute to “our general,” as his supporters cheered the “warrior of the Brazilian people.” The title was fittingly bestowed on André Janones, the 42-year-old congressman who vigorously mobilized almost 11 million voters as part of Lula’s “digital army,” marking the first time since Brazil’s return to democracy four decades ago that the runner-up surged ahead in the second round of a presidential election. It was a remarkable turnaround that will to be studied by election watchers for years to come.

In one of the most consequential elections in Brazilian history, some three-quarters of the population were getting their news from social media, and Meta—the owner of the most prominent platforms—was supportive of Sen. Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of the now-imprisoned former president. Lula’s campaign was allegedly thwarted online on Meta’s platforms. United Nations experts described as interruptions of “one side’s” social media accounts, curtailed audience engagement, and choked user feeds.

Users reported that Meta’s artificial intelligence tools refused to generate campaign posters for Lula, whose name would temporarily not appear in searches, while Bolsonaro’s profile was recommended. Leaked details revealed that Meta’s employees confirmed internal manipulations tilting the digital field in Bolsonaro’s favor, benefiting his conservative political movement. Noting its failures to stem disinformation in Brazil’s last elections, the company’s own oversight board issued its concern over “recurring enforcement failures in Meta’s election integrity efforts.”

What overcame the algorithm was Janones’s “Lula’s Army” WhatsApp channel, which swelled into the millions as the second round approached. Voters were mobilized as “soldiers,” enthusiastically showing their support through storms of emojis, and they were then dispatched on “missions” to mobilize more people. This effort helped mobilize more than 10 million supporters for Lula in the second round.

Unlike Meta’s other platforms such as Instagram and Facebook, WhatsApp is less susceptible to algorithmic control. Human beings, not algorithms, share what they want to share. Lula’s campaign recalled the original promise of technology as a force to connect, inform, and mobilize. As India’s Cockroach movement and Serbia’s student movement have recently demonstrated, digital tools can be powerfully used to help people reclaim their voices offline.

Brazil’s presidential election underscores not only the power of people to bring change, but also the danger that digital chokepoints, created by the power of a single tech company, can shape who gets heard and who doesn’t. Brazil’s is not a solitary case of Meta’s platforms influencing the result of a major election, or of voters mounting a countereffort. Asked for comment, Meta referred to a statement claiming that any impediments were not deliberate, but rather the result of technical glitches, and perhaps negligence. They also highlighted a Nov. 11 statement from Brazil’s electoral authority that it found no “deliberate action to skew the election.”

But these claims fail to reckon with the company’s own announcements that it is discarding fact-checkers and loosening controls on recommended political content. What’s more, at the same time that Brazil’s Supreme Court was regulating social media platforms, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerburg said he was working with the Trump administration to “push back” against “censorship,” disdainfully referring to Latin American “secret courts” that can order companies to take down content in the name of moderation and integrity.

Whether or not this interference was intentional, what matters is that it was possible. A single company can now stand between citizens and the representatives that they should freely elect, controlling who gets to be seen or who can be heard. The concentration of such power in the hands of so few becomes a chokepoint that can threaten democracy, even democracy itself as a political system.

In response, it will not be enough to add layers of regulation or ask Meta and other tech companies to do better. When these avenues have been pursued, they encounter hard limits. In India, Meta’s moderation of hate speech has been scrutinized; in Myanmar and Sri Lanka, rights groups accused Facebook of inflaming crimes against the Rohingya and other Muslim minorities; in Ethiopia, its algorithms amplified ethnic violence.

Two years ago, the European Commission opened still ongoing formal proceedings against Meta’s platforms under the Digital Services Act, and in the United States in recent months, it was subject to billion-dollar penalties for failing to address child safety and other problems around content moderation. In every case, enforcement has been slow, inadequate, ineffective, or too opaque to track or evaluate.

The infrastructure needed to regulate Big Tech is politically compromised. In the United States, Brazil, and other large markets, the industry runs a vast policy operation that runs deep into legislatures, think tanks, and lobbyists. There is also a regulation trap: The same politicians who we count on to regulate technology are the ones who depend on the tech companies to campaign, communicate, and fundraise.

Brazil now has some of the strongest regulation against electoral falsehoods after its election four years ago was marred by disinformation, culminating in an attempted coup carried out by those who argued—without evidence—that the electronic voting system was compromised. Regulation has since been taken up by the courts, with the Supreme Court finding tech companies’ immunity from liability for third-party content unconstitutional and the Superior Electoral Court demanding platform compliance plans.

The Lula administration also empowered public authorities to investigate platforms, enforce compliance, and ensure transparency, including in the form of specific decrees preventing digital violence against women. Today, Brazil is a rare case of a country in a position to act before the election; most others do not have strong enough institutions.

Tech companies are powerful political actors that exert potentially decisive political influence, which can be wielded for ideological or partisan ends, as in Brazil, where engagement-maximizing algorithms reward polarizing, inflammatory, and populist content. The answer must be to challenge that power, which can be done through three proposals for reform.

What Mark Zuckerberg’s change of heart on misinformation means for the world.

Brazil’s left-wing president won a historic fourth term in office.

The first step is to reduce society’s dependence on one or a few companies that have the capability to dominate the political or digital landscape. Here, competition law can be enforced, so that a single actor cannot sweep up a whole range of apps and close them off to rivals. Brazil’s public payment system Pix, for example, broke the dominance of private card systems by charging lower merchant fees and zero cost to customers. Because the central bank required all other banks to integrate Pix into their existing mobile apps, it also expanded digital financial inclusion for hundreds of millions.

Second, our societies’ digital architecture needs to be overhauled and constructed anew around open protocols. These are publicly documented technical standards that allow different services, applications, and devices to communicate using shared rules rather than through proprietary systems. Lula’s digital army demonstrated that they can be used or designed at scale for the public interest, in line with democratic principles.

The third is to build public options. Unregulated markets cannot be expected to produce fair outcomes that benefit everyone. Just as governments use their power as investors in the realms of defense, energy, semiconductors, and other infrastructure, they can also procure open-source software and open standards for their own services. Industrial policy and public funding can unlock alternatives to Big Tech companies. They can also help build open-weight and public interest AI models, which will provide access to local developers.

Elections show how high the stakes can be, but they represent only the most visible part of a wider struggle for truth, voice, and the fundamentals of human dignity. At its core, dignity, in the digital age, depends on people’s ability to have access to reliable knowledge, remain free from manipulation, and challenge decisions that shape their families. It demands regulations ensuring that everyone is treated equally; that they are protected from digital violence; and that they are free to live without being watched, profiled, and manipulated. None of these essential elements of human dignity can be left to the goodwill of a single corporation.

Equally worrying is that we are beginning to normalize the intense, aggressive digital messaging that bombards voters as part of electoral races—for better or for worse. Digital campaigning has a place to strengthen democratic participation and promote free and fair elections, but we are also seeing that it can cross the line in distorting, suppressing, and damaging the will of the people.

What Lula honored in his victory speech was what the algorithm could not block—namely, the resolve of millions to have their voices heard and their votes to be counted. Yet by virtue of their concentrated power and platforms through which public discourse occurs, tech companies will continue to be a chokepoint on democratic governance, so countries must learn not simply to regulate but more importantly to build an open, democratic digital architecture that works for all. Otherwise, these decisions will be made by someone else.

Binaifer Nowrojee is president of the Open Society Foundations, the world’s largest private funder working to promote human rights, equity, and justice.

Japan Treasury Sell-Off Raises Fears of Wider Global Market Turmoil

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Yash is a crypto analyst specializing in price analysis, predictions, and in-depth research reports. He combines technical indicators with on-chain data to uncover market trends and potential breakouts. His sharp insights help readers navigate the crypto market with confidence. Whether it’s Bitcoin or emerging altcoins, Yash breaks it down with clarity and precision.

Japan is reportedly selling more than ¥5.65 trillion in U.S. Treasuries amid currency and bond-market pressures.

China is reportedly reducing Treasury exposure while increasing gold reserves.

Growing Treasury sales could increase pressure on global bond markets, although a financial collapse isn't established.

Japan’s reported Treasury sell-off is adding another layer of anxiety to an already tense global financial picture. Claims of more than ¥5.65 trillion in U.S. Treasury sales, mounting bond losses, and rising gold reserves have raised questions about the future of traditional dollar-denominated reserves. And China is reportedly following a similar strategy.

The claims suggest Japan is facing mounting financial pressure after its stock market reportedly entered bear-market territory, with losses exceeding 15%. Meanwhile, bond losses are estimated at more than ¥30 trillion.

Japan is reportedly selling U.S. Treasuries to support the yen and defend its currency. The reported liquidation exceeds ¥5.65 trillion, while its gold holdings have climbed to an unprecedented high.

However, selling dollar-denominated assets doesn’t automatically mean abandoning the dollar. Currency intervention, reserve management, and domestic financial stability can all influence these decisions.

🚨 WARNING: SOMETHING REALLY BAD JUST HAPPENEDJapan's stock market has officially entered a BEAR MARKET after crashing over 15%.The country is now facing over ¥30 TRILLION in bond losses.To prevent a total collapse, Japan has dumped over ¥5.65 TRILLION in U.S. Treasuries.… pic.twitter.com/7lwR42tsq3

Japan isn’t the only country mentioned in this changing reserve narrative. China is also reportedly reducing its U.S. Treasury exposure while expanding gold reserves.

The parallel is attracting attention. Both economies are described as prioritizing gold accumulation and reducing reliance on conventional dollar-denominated assets.

The post also point to Chinese AI stocks losing almost 50% over several months, adding another source of market anxiety. However, the connection between AI stock declines and Treasury liquidation hasn’t been established.

The bigger concern is what happens if Treasury selling accelerates. Large-scale liquidations could create additional pressure across bond markets, while currency interventions and rising gold purchases could reshape investor expectations.

Still, predictions of an imminent historic market crash go beyond the figures provided. Japan and China may be adjusting their financial positions, but that alone doesn’t establish a systemic collapse.

For now, the Japan Treasury sell-off narrative deserves attention, particularly alongside reported gold accumulation and currency pressures. Whether these developments signal a lasting shift away from dollar reserves remains an open question.

AI SRE may help you fight fires, but Foresight AI helps before the spark

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI SRE tools help teams recover once something breaks. Gremlin Foresight AI finds reliability risks, delivers fixes and checks that they work before customers notice.

Artificial intelligence is fundamentally changing how software is built, deployed, and managed. AI coding assistants are helping developers ship software faster than ever. Meanwhile, a new generation of AI-powered Site Reliability Engineering (SRE) tools promises to help organizations respond to incidents and recover from outages in record time.

But there’s a problem with this approach: By the time an AI SRE tool springs into action, something has already gone wrong.

What if AI could help prevent the incident from happening in the first place?

That’s the idea behind Gremlin Foresight AI, a new agentic reliability solution from Gremlin, the Chaos Engineering company. Rather than waiting for systems to fail and using AI to accelerate recovery, Foresight AI proactively identifies reliability risks and recommends and delivers fixes. It then validates that those fixes work, all before customers experience an outage.

The distinction represents an important shift in how organizations think about AI and software reliability. Instead of simply getting better at fighting fires, Gremlin wants to eliminate the conditions that cause them.

The rise of AI-powered software development has created an interesting paradox for engineering organizations. Developers can now generate and deploy code at unprecedented speeds. But that acceleration also creates more opportunities for bugs, configuration errors, and architectural weaknesses to find their way into production.

Meanwhile, the operational complexity of modern applications continues to grow. Cloud-native architectures, microservices, distributed infrastructure, and third-party dependencies have created increasingly interconnected systems where a seemingly minor issue can cascade into a major outage.

For years, organizations have invested heavily in observability, monitoring, and incident management tools to address these challenges. More recently, AI SRE solutions have emerged to automate troubleshooting, identify root causes, and help engineers resolve incidents faster.

These capabilities are valuable. Reducing the time it takes to recover from an outage can significantly limit its financial and operational impact.

But AI SRE largely addresses the consequences of a reliability failure rather than preventing the failure itself.

Gremlin believes that distinction is becoming increasingly important as AI accelerates the pace of software development.

“AI-driven development means shipping code at 10X velocity; it also means 10x the opportunity for bugs, risks, and failures,” said Kolton Andrus, CEO and founder of Gremlin. “While the rise of AI SRE tools are great for helping teams respond to incidents faster, it’s still cleanup after something breaks. Gremlin Foresight AI finds risks proactively, delivers the fix, and verifies the fix worked by rerunning the test. This provides teams with the assurances needed to move confidently.”

The philosophy behind Foresight AI isn’t entirely new. Gremlin has spent years helping organizations proactively discover weaknesses in their systems through Chaos Engineering. It is a discipline that deliberately introduces controlled failures to understand how applications behave under adverse conditions.

The challenge has always been making that process accessible and repeatable.

Traditional Chaos Engineering often requires specialized expertise, considerable engineering effort, and time that development teams don’t always have. Engineers must determine which failure scenarios to test, interpret the results, identify potential fixes, and verify that the changes actually improve resilience.

Gremlin designed Foresight AI to automate much of that work.

The platform uses specialized AI agents to analyze applications, identify potential failure scenarios, execute controlled reliability tests, and recommend targeted remediations. These fixes can take the form of configuration patches or infrastructure-as-code changes that fit into existing engineering workflows.

Importantly, the process doesn’t end when a fix is proposed or implemented.

Foresight AI reruns the original reliability test to verify that the weakness has actually been addressed. It can then continue testing as applications and infrastructure evolve, helping organizations identify regressions before they become production incidents.

This creates a continuous cycle of identifying risks, fixing weaknesses, and validating resilience.

Consider an application that relies on infrastructure deployed in a single cloud availability zone. Everything may appear healthy under normal operating conditions. Monitoring dashboards are green, application performance is acceptable, and customers aren’t reporting problems.

But if that availability zone fails, the application could become unavailable.

An AI SRE tool might help diagnose the resulting outage and accelerate recovery. Gremlin designed Foresight AI to identify the architectural weakness beforehand and recommend changes that improve redundancy. It then tests whether the application can withstand the failure.

The difference is between recovering from a preventable outage and avoiding it altogether.

One of the more interesting aspects of Foresight AI is the technology behind its recommendations.

While many AI-powered operations tools rely heavily on large language models to interpret telemetry, logs, and incident histories. Gremlin has built Foresight AI around its proprietary Failure Atlas.

The Failure Atlas draws on more than a decade of real-world reliability testing, including millions of fault-injection experiments conducted across distributed systems.

That historical knowledge provides Foresight AI with a specialized foundation for understanding how applications fail, which failure conditions are most consequential, and what kinds of remediation are likely to improve resilience.

Foresight AI doesn’t rely solely on an LLM to infer what might go wrong. It combines information about an organization’s actual infrastructure with Gremlin’s accumulated knowledge of system failures and recovery patterns.

The AI agents use that context to recommend relevant tests, interpret outcomes, and develop actionable remediation plans.

This is an important distinction in an increasingly crowded AI infrastructure market. General-purpose language models can offer plausible explanations for technical problems, but reliability engineering requires more than plausible answers. It requires evidence that a system will continue functioning when something goes wrong.

Gremlin’s approach attempts to provide that evidence through controlled experimentation and repeatable validation.

Foresight AI also introduces an agentic approach to reliability engineering, with specialized agents responsible for different aspects of the process.

An Analyst agent examines applications and infrastructure to identify potential weaknesses and recommend appropriate tests. A Tester agent executes controlled experiments to determine how systems behave under failure conditions. An Operator agent interprets the results and provides specific remediation recommendations.

A fourth agent, acting as a Technical Program Manager, helps organizations coordinate reliability initiatives across teams. It also identifies gaps in testing and tracks progress over time.

Together, these agents aim to reduce the manual work traditionally associated with reliability engineering.

Rather than requiring engineers to become experts in every possible failure scenario, Foresight AI helps determine where the risks are, which tests to perform, and what actions are necessary to improve resilience.

Human oversight remains part of the process. Engineers retain approval authority over testing and remediation, allowing organizations to introduce automation without relinquishing control over production infrastructure.

The broader objective is to make proactive reliability engineering a continuous operational practice rather than an occasional exercise conducted by a small group of specialists.

One of the longstanding challenges of reliability engineering is demonstrating the business value of prevention.

When an outage occurs, its impact is often obvious. Organizations can measure downtime, lost revenue, recovery costs, and customer disruption.

But how do you quantify the value of an outage that never happened?

Foresight AI addresses this challenge by incorporating reliability scoring, continuous validation, and reporting that helps organizations measure improvements across applications and teams.

Engineering leaders don’t have to rely exclusively on traditional operational metrics such as mean time to recovery. They can track which risks have been identified, which weaknesses have been remediated, and whether systems continue to pass resilience tests as they change.

This gives organizations a more tangible way to evaluate their reliability investments and demonstrate progress.

It also reflects a broader evolution in the discipline of Site Reliability Engineering. It is moving beyond measuring how effectively teams respond to failure toward measuring how systematically they reduce the likelihood and impact of failure.

Gremlin’s launch of Foresight AI arrives at a time when the relationship between software development velocity and operational reliability is becoming increasingly important.

AI coding tools are accelerating the creation of software. AI SRE tools are accelerating the diagnosis and resolution of incidents. But neither development speed nor recovery speed, on its own, guarantees that applications will become more resilient.

Proactive reliability engineering offers a complementary approach: continuously testing systems, identifying weaknesses, and addressing risks before they affect customers.

The real test for Foresight AI will be how effectively it can translate its knowledge of failure patterns into reliable, actionable fixes across the diverse environments enterprises operate. Identifying a weakness is one challenge; demonstrating that a proposed remediation works safely and consistently is another.

For years, the technology industry has focused on reducing the time it takes to detect and recover from outages. Gremlin is making the case that the next major advance in AI-powered operations will come from reducing the number of outages organizations experience in the first place.

AI SRE may help organizations fight fires faster. With Foresight AI, Gremlin is helping enterprises assess risk and address issues before the spark.

Finland Allows Tesla FSD, So Long As It's Not Called That

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Happy Wednesday! It's November 11, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at Finland approving FSD by any other name, and Volkswagen's successes in China. We'll also look at VW's future plans in the country, and Nissan's profit targets.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Tesla desperately wants its FSD software approved in Europe, but so far it's only been adopted piecemeal. The latest adopter? Finland, which approved the tech with some slight modifications — but said that it may not vote the same way in an EU-wide decision. From Reuters:

STOCKHOLM, Nov 11 (Reuters) – Finland ‌granted provisional approval on Wednesday for Tesla's Assisted Driving system (TAD) after the US automaker made changes to the technology, allowing its use on Finnish roads ahead of an EU-wide ​vote.

Nearly a dozen countries, including Slovakia and Belgium, have granted provisional approvals for ​the technology, which allows vehicles to steer, accelerate and brake automatically ⁠under driver supervision.

However, Tesla has yet to secure backing from any of ​the EU's largest member states, whose support is likely to be crucial to ​winning approval across the bloc.

Finland's transport authority, Traficom, said it ​approved the system after Tesla renamed it from "Full Self-Driving" and tightened speed-control settings ​that critics said encouraged speeding.

FSD is, really, Tesla's flagship at the moment. The Roadster isn't real, the Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck are all dead, the Semi only sort of exists for fleet sales, and the Optimus robot will likely never be a household product. Tesla's big thing is its software, and it can't sell that software in a major economic bloc. Not a great look for a company that pretends not to be a car company — though at this point, with only two real offerings, maybe it's no longer pretending.

China has been a rough market for most automakers recently, with the country's consumers spending less and preferring to dedicate the cash they do spend on domestic brands. Volkswagen, though, is doing just fine. In fact, it just set a sales record in the country. From Automotive News:

Volkswagen Group said its namesake brand's sales of electric vehicles and hybrids in China climbed to a record last month and forecast that growth will continue this year as its turnaround efforts in the world's largest auto market start bearing fruit.

October deliveries of VW EVs and plug-in hybrids surged nearly 350 percent from a year ago to more than 28,000 vehicles, the group said Nov. 11. New models including the ID Aura T6 SUV and the ID Era 5S sedan drove the increase.

The company said it expects to sustain growth in the coming months, with a goal of ranking among the 10 best-selling New Energy Vehicle brands in the country next year.

Of course, those sales goals could be stymied by our next gear.

Despite recent sales success, Volkswagen doesn't seem to have much invested in the future of its Chinese wing. In fact, the brand isn't even working on new models for the Chinese market — at least, not new models due out before 2029. From Reuters:

BEIJING, Nov 11 (Reuters) – Volkswagen's electric-vehicle joint venture in China has not planned any new models for 2027 or 2028, two people with knowledge ​of the situation said, after it banked on far greater demand for its ID. UNYX series than what has materialised.

Without new models, money-losing ‌Volkswagen Anhui is stuck with a line-up that has not caught on with the young, urban consumers it targeted when it launched the UNYX marque in July 2024, in a market where automakers introduce models and variants on a monthly basis, with hundreds launched so far this year.

Reuters' characterization of Volkswagen Anhui as having "not caught on" seems a bit much, with the brand setting its own sales records. Even if it's growing, though, it's still small — small enough that VW corporate may not want to risk putting good money after bad.

Speaking of sales being down in China, Nissan's not doing so hot in the country. Its profit goals are undeterred, though, as the company's CEO expects sales in Japan and the U.S. to skyrocket in place of China. From Automotive News:

YOKOHAMA, Japan — Nissan CEO Ivan Espinosa is betting on rising sales in the U.S. and Japan to keep earnings on track after the crumbling China market undercut quarterly performance and forced him to downgrade the company's global sales forecast for the second time this year.

Nissan Motor Co. upgraded its sales target for the key markets of Japan and the U.S. for the current fiscal year ending March 31 citing the arrival of new product such as the Rogue crossover.

But the uptick won't be enough to offset the China slump, with Nissan forecasting global sales to fall to between 2.97 million to 3 million vehicles this fiscal year, from 3.15 million last year.

We haven't seen U.S. car sales tank like China yet, but it does feel like it's on the horizon — cars keep getting pricier and buyers keep getting broker. Eventually, something has to give. Hopefully it doesn't kill Nissan on the way.

What happened to you, journalism industry? You used to be cool. I don't mean the drugs part, I mean the "paying people a livable wage to write stuff like that" part.

Under $4! This happened yesterday, so Andy got the big celebration, but it's worth mentioning today too. Back to brunch, everyone.

How To Rob A Bank Producer Explains How The Movie Reinvents The Heist Genre [Exclusive]

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's easy for novices to make the mistake that if you've seen one heist film, you've seen them all. As the genre junkies among us know, it takes a little more effort than most to make these sorts of movies stand out from the crowd. Steven Soderbergh found lightning in a bottle with his "Ocean's Eleven" remake in 2001, while Christopher Nolan added a mind-bending spin with "Inception" less than 10 years later. Classic cinema is rife with examples of how to craft memorable heist movies, from Michael Mann's "Thief" to Sidney Lumet's "Dog Day Afternoon" to Stanley Kubrick's "The Killing."

So when David Leitch came across writer Mark Bianculli's script for "How to Rob a Bank" and chose this as his next directorial effort after "The Fall Guy," it quickly became apparent how this could become a heist flick like nothing we've ever seen before. As the title implies, the unconventional heroes of this story are a group of social media influencers who've become viral sensations — not just for pulling off bank robberies like they were born to do it, but for creating a YouTube channel teaching others how to stick it to the man and perform heists of their own.

Not long ago, I had the chance to talk to the creative team of Leitch and "Violent Night" producer Kelly McCormick (who happens to be Leitch's longtime producing partner at 87North, as well as his wife) during the "How to Rob a Bank" press junket about what drew them to the script. According to McCormick:

"In this case, it's like, 'How do you turn a heist movie on its head — not your daddy's heist movie?' And it's the [idea of] thieves hiding in plain sight, as opposed to not wanting to be seen."

It's a staple of practically every heist story ever told, from "The Italian Job" to "Heat" and everything in between: In order to pull off the perfect caper, our protagonists must cover their tracks, don some clever disguises to maintain a low profile, and escape the scene of the crime without anyone being the wiser. It's definitely not advised for robbers to film themselves while on a job, edit and upload those tutorials to YouTube, and revel in the viral fame that comes with being the most well-known crew the internet has ever seen. It's all about avoiding detection — not seeking it out.

But "How to Rob a Bank" isn't exactly interested in the conventional approach. With Nicholas Hoult's Ryan as the ringleader, this motley group of Robin Hood-like revolutionaries have figured out how to successfully weaponize simmering anti-capitalist sentiments among a generation fed up with living under a broken system. They just happen to do so with a GoPro strapped to their masks and a knack for live-streaming their crimes to an eager audience. According to director David Leitch, this was the main appeal behind taking the screenplay by Mark Bianculli and bringing it to life:

"That's what we saw in it and that's why we really wanted to do this material. It had all of those elements where you could create something fun, but yet also with a bigger message. That was one of the things we haven't gotten to do: maybe have something that has a little bit more of a commentary. There's usually a moral tale [in heist movies], but this has a little bit of ... it's asking you big questions about where we are in the world right now."

Where studios like A24 and Blumhouse deserve credit for establishing themselves as high-minded horror destinations, 87North has cornered the market with the most exciting action films. Upon seeing David Leitch's name attached to any given film, audiences can safely expect more visceral set pieces, an emphasis on practical effects, and a certain, undefinable je ne sais quoi when it comes to mixing and matching genres. After "Atomic Blonde," "Deadpool 2," and "Bullet Train," "How to Rob a Bank" seamlessly fits into Leitch's ongoing interests.

Or, as Kelly McCormick aptly puts it in our conversation, "I believe one of David's pillars is that he always is doing a little bit something different with the genre every time out, and wants to and enjoys changing genres and the subgenres within action and really wanting to tell a lot of different tales." It's one thing to identify that branding, however, and another to actually put it into action. As she went on to explain:

"So then, how can you make that Leitchian? How can you make that atonal? And ['How to Rob a Bank'] had all the things, with regards to the drama versus the social media content and all those kinds of aspects. And then, I think the extra unique part is being able to have a bigger message, more front-and-center than what we do a lot of times out with our commercial propositions."

That "bigger message" may hit particularly hard at a time when working-class struggles remain at the forefront of the national conversation as the rich only get richer. "How to Rob a Bank" hits theaters on November 13, 2026.

Bitcoin Core Breaks Silence on Three Patched Security Vulnerabilities

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin Core developers have revealed three key security vulnerabilities, including a high-severity flaw that could potentially disrupt the mining capabilities of the Bitcoin network, preventing miners from creating new blocks.

As revealed in security advisories published on Nov. 10, the vulnerabilities impacted various elements of the Bitcoin Core software that powers many nodes underpinning the world's leading cryptocurrency.

One of the vulnerabilities, titled CVE-2025-62861, was rated as a high-severity issue due to its potential effect on Bitcoin miners.

The bug has the potential to create problems after a blockchain reorganization, which is when previously accepted blocks are replaced by the network with an alternative chain.

More precisely, the issue could cause an inconsistent state in the node's mempool, where all unconfirmed transactions are stored.

The vulnerability impacted Bitcoin Core software versions 28.0-28.2 and 29.0-29.1.

It was first reported on Sept. 26, 2025, by security researcher Niklas Goegge.

Beyond the mining vulnerability, Bitcoin Core developers have announced two further security problems, rated medium severity.

The first one (CVE-2025-62859) could enable malicious network participants to slow down Bitcoin block processing through an exploit that involves changes in transaction witness data.

Under some circumstances, it could make an affected node wait for up to 10 minutes before processing a block.

Moreover, such an attack could be repeated indefinitely on the node when the latter is running in specific configurations.

The vulnerability was found by Eugene Siegel in April 2025.

The second vulnerability (CVE-2025-62860) involved Bitcoin nodes that operate on the anonymous Tor network.

In fact, the Bitcoin Core developers revealed that thousands of reachable Bitcoin mainnet nodes had been granting permissions for incoming Tor connections.

Nevertheless, the issue did not affect nodes that do not use the optional whitelist configuration.

The three vulnerabilities are not new discoveries.

In fact, all of the issues were already fixed by the Bitcoin Core developers in October 2025.

The fixes were integrated into both Bitcoin Core 30.0, which was released on Oct. 10, and Bitcoin Core 29.2, rolled out on Oct. 14.

The high-severity mining vulnerability was also patched in Bitcoin Core 28.3.

The delay in the disclosure of the vulnerabilities was in line with the Bitcoin Core security policy, which usually calls for developers to wait at least two weeks after the end of life of the last affected software version before publishing details of medium- or high-severity vulnerabilities.

Whether you enjoy invading other players and trolling them or helping people out in particularly hefty encounters, all is now well once again across the planes of Drangleic and Lothric. "Some of the best games of the last decade came from teams of fifty": Destroy All Humans! remake devs Black Forest Games split from THQ Nordic to launch new indie studio group

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"We're excited to shape our own future as owners again"

Black Forest Games, developers of the Destroy All Humans! remakes, have spun out of Embracer Group-owned publishers THQ Nordic via a management buyout and announced plans to form a new "developer-driven group" of independent studios. The new group, dubbed Third Signal, will aim to "give teams the independence, resources and creative freedom" to "bring their own ideas to life".

Black Forest, based in Germany, announced the move in a press release. "We are grateful to THQ Nordic and Embracer for nine good years and the games we made together," co-boss Adrian Goersch said. "Now, we're excited to shape our own future as owners again, to work on an exciting new project with a great team and strong partners behind us."

"Some of the best games of the last decade came from teams of fifty, not five hundred," added Third Signal founder Andreas Tobler. "Black Forest Games is one of them, and the right studio to launch Third Signal. Our vision is to build a developer driven group and give teams the independence, resources and creative freedom to continue their creative legacy and bring their own ideas to life." As of writing, Third Signal's website is just a logo splash which promises something coming soon. To be fair, it does also look to have an email address, plus legal and privacy notices. Black Forest's management team will become a co-owner of the studio group as part of this move, but "continue to collaborate on game development tasks" with THQ Nordic.

Black Forest had been in the embrace since 2017, releasing both 2020's Destroy All Humans! remake and Destroy All Humans! 2: Reprobed in that time, plus DLC Clone Carnage. Over the years they've also made multiple entries in the Giana Sisters platformer series, survival game Fade to Silence, and 2017's Bubsy: The Woolies Strike Back.

That's a right old mix of stuff, but the biggest names in it are all resurrections of series or characters created by other folks back in the day. Pandemic Studios in the case of Destroy All Humans!, Accolade in the case of Bubsy, and Germany company Time Warp in the case of Giana Sisters. That makes the fact it sounds like this move'll give Black Forest a bit more creative freedom intriguing. Here's hoping that if they do plan to try and get some more of their own original ideas off the ground, rather than trucking on with a steady diet of remakes and revivals, they manage to stick the landing. It's all fine declaring that a 50 person team can make a top game these days, but that certainly doesn't mean it's in any way easy to pull off.

It's the most magical time of the year, but it can be stressful knowing what to buy, especially if you're looking for a gift for a fitness fanatic. Whether you're shopping for the kind of runner who laps the block to round up his or her miles, or a cyclist who will bore you for hours about the pros and cons of tubeless tires, we've got you covered.

We've hand-picked some of the best gifts for the runner, cyclist, Hyrox-obsessed nephew, or Pilates princess in your life below, using suggestions from the Tom's Guide fitness team — we've run over 20 marathons between us, we spend our weekends hiking for fun, and we even have a personal trainer on staff, so we know what every fitness lover will be delighted to find under the tree this year.

We have separated the best gifts into different price categories, so you can shop whatever your budget. Happy shopping!

Better You Magnesium Flakes. There's nothing better than a hot bath after a chilly training run or gruelling weights session in the winter, and chucking some magnesium flakes in will help your runners' muscles recover faster. This bag of magnesium flakes is the perfect stocking filler, and will last them ages.

Brick Caps: Tyre Valve Dust CapsThe ultimate bike accessory you've probably not heard of. Brick Caps make some amazing dust caps including these lego ones, but also Star Wars characters.

Massage ball set As handy as one of the best foam rollers, these little massage balls are perfect for getting into tight muscles. The set comes with two spiky massage balls, as well as a mini massage ball for targeting small, hard-to-reach areas. All three balls come in a handy carry bag for chucking in a gym bag.

Nalgene 32 oz Wide Mouth Water BottleWhatever the fitness fanatic in your life is training for, this 32 ounce water bottle will keep them hydrated. It's super-easy to drink from, and much lighter than a lot of the metal bottles on the market. Plus, it's dishwasher-friendly.

Lock LacesThe runner or triathlete in your life is bound to appreciate it this gift. The clever Lock Laces mean your runner never has to worry about their shoelaces coming untied on race day, and will help your triathlete with faster transitions.

LED Safety LightsThese little lights are affordable and can help keep your runner safe and seen when the weather changes. They clip onto anything — from a backpack to a running jacket — and have different modes of flashing to help cars spot your runner on the move.

Motivational shoe tagsIf your runner is taking on her first race this year, these motivational shoe tags are a cute little stocking filler for less than $10. They slot into most laces, and will help give your runner a boost when she needs it on race day.

Hot yoga towelA gift the yogi or Pilates-lover in your life will appreciate. This handy towel folds up to nothing, but will give them extra grip during practice. The perfect stocking filler!

Body Glide gift setThis might seem like an odd gift, but this handy bundle of anti-chafe balms can be rolled all over the body and feet to prevent any uncomfortable rubbing or blisters.

Peach Flow Studio Pilates socksThese cute little Pilates socks will give your loved one all the grip they need to get through their reformer class, and come in several adorable prints they are bound to love.

Honey Stinger WaffleNothing says good luck with your next challenge like a box of Honey Stinger waffles. Whether it's an Ironman or a Hyrox, your fitness fanatic will love them.

Yeti Rambler Stackable CupWhatever morning workout class they are dashing to, this Yeti cup will keep their coffee warm. The magnet lid will also stop splashes when drinking on the go.

Sprigs ArmbandThe top pick in our best running phone holders round-up is a great gift for anyone who likes to bring their phone with them on the run. It's lightweight, fits securely and your phone won't bounce thanks to the top strap.

BlenderBottle Stainless Steel shaker Another slightly odd-sounding gift to most, but this clever bottle will be an instant hit with the gym lover in your life. It has a leak-proof lid, and will keep cold drinks cold for 24 hours.

Ciele GoCapThe Ciele GoCap is a comfortable, five-panel cap that comes in a wide range of styles, so you're sure to find one that suits your runner. We've been using Ciele caps regularly for years and they are durable, easy to wash, and a cut above most other running caps.

Spibelt Running BeltThe SPIbelt made it onto our list of the best running belts due to its great price and versatility. The belt looks like a thin pocket with an adjustable strap and is far more compact than a lot of the other belts on the market. Plus, it expands to fit all your runner's essentials on the move.

Tracksmith Inverno GlovesThese gloves are truly excellent. They are breathable enough that your runner won't have to remove them after their warm-up, soft enough that they can wipe their runny nose with ease, and they even have tech fingers to allow them to answer their phone or take a selfie without having to remove the gloves.

Goodr SunglassesGoodr's range of affordable sunglasses are rightly very popular with runners and cyclists thanks to their stylish designs which stay put even as you get sweaty.

Supergoop! Everyday Play SPF 50 LotionSPF is an absolute essential whether you're playing tennis, heading for a hike, or running a marathon. This Supergoop! Play lotion is my go-to all year round. It doesn't irritate my eyes when I sweat or leave me with gross white marks on my face.

Fast and Free Fleece Running Ear WarmerKeep cold ears toasty all winter long with this sweat-wicking ear warmer. It's nice and fleecy on the inside, making it soft against the skin, and even has a gap for your runner's ponytail. Available in several different colors.

Discord teases long-awaited mobile feature as it prepares major app upgrade

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Discord has confirmed that its long-awaited mobile account switcher is on the way, giving users the ability to move between multiple accounts without repeatedly logging in and out.

The feature will arrive as part of Discord’s new Rust-based client, which is currently being tested with a focus on improving performance across devices.

While Discord already offers account switching on desktop, mobile users have been waiting for the same functionality. Now, the company has shared an early look at how it will work on smartphones.

On November 10, Discord developer Stanislav shared a video demonstrating the account switcher running on an iPhone, showing how quickly users can move between profiles.

“Fast launch times are cool, but actual usage is what matters,” he wrote. “Check out the speed of the long-requested mobile account switcher on iOS on the new Rust client. And yes, it is just as fast on Android and Desktop.”

The new Rust client will feature the long-awaited mobile account switcher https://t.co/R5XUj8H5Bh

The demonstration shows a user opening Discord’s account menu and switching between profiles in a matter of seconds, without needing to manually enter login credentials each time.

Discord subsequently shared the video through its official X account, confirming that the new Rust client will include the long-awaited feature.

The account switcher could be particularly useful for people who maintain separate Discord profiles for personal conversations, gaming communities, or other activities.

Beyond account switching, the new client is also being developed with performance in mind. Stanislav’s comments suggest that Discord is focusing on improving both launch times and everyday responsiveness rather than simply making the app faster to open.

Discord reveals major app overhaul that could make it faster and use less ram

Discord reveals age-check rollout with massive changes to how it works

Discord rolls out huge batch of highly requested features after asking users for ideas

However, users will have to wait a little longer before trying the feature themselves.

“We’re still in testing for now but hope to get it out to you as soon as we can,” Stanislav added.

The mobile account switcher is the latest in a series of changes Discord has made in response to user feedback. In September, the platform rolled out several highly requested features after receiving more than 3,000 suggestions from its community, including the ability to pin DMs on desktop, expanded profile customization, and new moderation tools.

Discord also confirmed it was working on additional features, including larger Group DMs and the ability to hide profile badges. Those improvements are part of a broader effort to address community requests, with the company saying more updates are planned for the future.

Brazil electoral court fines Meta, sees no deliberate election bias

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The fine came after President Lula's coalition filed a complaint. The court said there was no evidence of intent to influence the election. Meta said it will defend itself against "false allegations."

Brazil's top electoral court has ordered Meta to pay 7.5 million reais ($1.34 million; €1.22 million) for failing to fully comply with judicial orders during last month's presidential election.

However, the judicial body has so far found no evidence that Meta deliberately acted to influence the election outcome.

Ahead of the runoff vote on October 25, leftist President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva's coalition filed a complaint, accusing Meta of "algorithmic manipulation" favoring his far-right rival Flavio Bolsonaro.

Platforms owned by Meta include Facebook, Instagram and WhatsApp.

Meta told the court last month that it had implemented measures to guarantee equal treatment for both campaigns.

The Superior Electoral Court (TSE) unanimously ruled on Tuesday to impose the 7.5 million reais fine on the US tech giant.

Meta missed deadlines and lacked transparency in its investigation, the court said.

On the other hand, the court's reporting judge Marco Aurelio Belizze wrote in his ruling that there were no preliminary evidence indicating that any intentional actions were taken to influence the election.

"That ​conclusion would require robust evidence, to be produced through the appropriate legal proceedings," he said.

According to Bellizze, the fine was based on Meta's overall handling of the court orders, including delays, incomplete disclosures, and challenges in verifying some of the information it provided.

In a statement on Wednesday, Meta strongly denied the court's ruling.

"We will continue to present the facts and defend ourselves against false allegations in the ‌ongoing ​legal proceedings," Meta said in a statement on Wednesday.

Lula defeated Bolsonaro in a tight run-off in October 25 with him winning 51.7% of the vote against Bolsonaro's 48.2%.

In his victory speech, Lula urged more regulation of big tech platforms after allegations that Meta interfered in the race.

"We had to overcome foreign interference in the election, coming from outside through digital platforms that we will have to regulate," he said.

What happened at Weil Gotshal? Could tech help other firms avoid that fate?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

November 11, 2026, 9:17 am CST By Victor Li

But when Weil, Gotshal & Manges announced last month that it would be winding down operations, it felt different compared to the others before.

After all, many of the BigLaw firms that went out of the business during the Great Recession had been overexposed in certain areas, and when the housing market crashed, so did their business. Dewey’s failure happened for a lot of reasons, mainly due to big money guarantees to partners, an ill-advised merger, and alleged financial irregularities that resulted in criminal indictments.

Weil Gotshal, though, had long been a firm with one of the best reputations on Wall Street, let alone the world. How did a firm like that end up joining the others on the dustheap? And could technology play a role in helping other firms avoid such a fate in the future?

In this episode of the Legal Rebels Podcast, Noah Waisberg talks to the ABA Journal’s Victor Li about those and other topics. Waisberg is the co-founder and the CEO of Zuva—an artificial intelligence contract analysis platform—and a longtime advocate of using technology to review contracts. He’s also a former Weil Gotshal associate, having worked there as a summer associate in 2005 and then as an associate from 2006 to 2010.

Before leading Zuva, Waisberg was the CEO of Kira Systems, the leading contract analysis company for professional service agencies, from its founding to its sale to Litera in 2021.

Seattle VC firm Ascend closes $17M fund to back ‘Third Act’ startups in physical AI and hardware

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 11, 2026 at 7:15 amNovember 11, 2026 at 7:30 am

Venture firm Ascend has closed a $17 million third fund and landed its first institutional investor, in what founder Kirby Winfield calls the toughest fundraising market for small venture firms in 15 years.

The Seattle-based firm invests at the earliest stage, often writing the first check into a startup. It was the first investor in Overland AI, the maker of autonomous military vehicles, and an early backer of subsea geothermal startup Endurance Energy, among others.

Ascend Fund III will back more physical AI startups building robots, energy systems, defense technology and other ventures that blend software and hardware — part of a larger bet on what the Ascend team likes to call Seattle’s “Third Act.”

Winfield said raising a third fund in this market shows that Ascend has “arrived on the national stage,” along with Seattle’s rise as a hub for this new class of startups. He said the firm’s track record puts it in the same league as the country’s top seed investors.

“We are not just some nice guys in the corner of the country helping out our neighbors,” said Winfield, who works closely with Jen Haller, Ascend partner and chief of staff, and Nate Bek, a principal at the firm.

Who’s backing the fund: The new institutional investor is FirstLook Partners, a firm that invests in smaller venture funds. Josh Porter, FirstLook’s co-founder and managing partner, said the firm, which is “very bullish on the Seattle ecosystem,” looked at several startup markets outside San Francisco and New York and met many investors in Seattle, “but all roads kept leading back to Kirby.”

Porter said Winfield has “lived the founder journey” and has “the operational chops to sit across the table from a founder and empathize with what they’re going through.”

The rest of the fund comes from family offices, founders who have sold companies, and more than a dozen founders of Ascend portfolio companies.

A tough market for small funds: Ascend’s raise comes as smaller venture firms struggle to bring in new money. Their investors, mostly wealthy individuals and family offices, are waiting longer for returns from earlier funds.

Just 211 newer venture firms closed at least one fund through the end of September, down from 927 in all of 2022, according to the PitchBook-NVCA Venture Monitor. Established firms took a record 88.2% of the money raised by U.S. venture funds in the first nine months of the year, the report found. Porter said smaller firms have to show “a right to exist and a right to win deals.”

Ascend launched its first fund in 2019 and ultimately raised $15 million. Its second fund closed at $25 million in 2023. The firm generally operates in the $15 million to $25 million range and writes the same size checks regardless of fund size, Winfield said.

“We think Kirby knows his place in the larger ecosystem, knows the value he brings to the table, and has sized his fund accordingly,” Porter said.

The firm has already drawn about 60% of the money committed to Fund III. GeekWire first reported that the firm was raising the fund in April 2025, based on filings at the time. Endurance was one of the firm’s first investments from the new fund, in mid-2025.

Ascend’s track record: Winfield started Ascend after two decades in leadership roles at four startups. He described the first fund as a proof of concept that “stumbled into some unicorns,” including Fabric, an e-commerce software company that was valued at $1.5 billion in 2022. Ascend was in its first round.

With its second fund, Ascend moved away from consumer and marketplace investing to focus on enterprise AI and deep tech. Its most successful investments from that fund, according to Winfield, include Overland AI, Sabi, Yoodli and Zocks.

After Ascend’s first checks, its portfolio companies have gone on to raise more than $1.5 billion from investors including Accel, Benchmark, Bessemer, Founders Fund, Khosla and Lightspeed, according to Ascend.

Ascend’s second and third funds rank in the top 10% of funds of $50 million or less, based on current valuations of their portfolio companies, Winfield said, citing figures Ascend shared with its investors at its annual meeting. The firm didn’t disclose specific returns or how much cash it has returned to its investors.

What Ascend looks for: The firm looks for ideas that seem too difficult, too boring, too confusing or too small for big multistage venture firms to evaluate. Ideally, Winfield said, its founders are doing something that makes Series A investors “a little bit nervous.”

That’s why Ascend hasn’t made many more defense tech investments since backing Overland, he said. Valuations for well-known founders in the sector have soared as more investors have piled in.

“The time to invest in defense tech was when nobody was investing in defense tech,” Winfield said.

Porter said Ascend’s focus on Seattle and physical AI is “really the bet we made with Ascend,” adding that Endurance and Overland have the potential to become “generational, category-defining companies.”

Fund III also backs AI software and infrastructure companies, including Seattle-area startup Govstream.ai, which uses AI to speed city permitting.

Seattle’s economic climate: Winfield is a champion of Seattle’s startup scene, known for Ascend’s annual Founders Bash party in the city and his ever-present ☔🔥☔ emojis on social media, symbolizing hot startups in rainy Seattle.

Ascend’s refreshed website and brand, launched with Fund III, nod to the firm’s Seattle roots with its longstanding Seattle Startup Toolkit and a Spotify playlist of Pacific Northwest indie rock and hip-hop.

But Winfield has also been a vocal critic of state and local policies he sees as hostile to startups, including Washington’s new tax on income over $1 million, which takes effect in 2028 and survived a repeal vote last week. He worries the tax could eventually be extended to people earning $150,000 a year, which would make it harder for startups to recruit employees from places like Texas.

“The chilling effect that’s going to have on the ecosystem is real, and it’s happening,” he said.

About 60% of Ascend’s portfolio companies are based in Seattle, according to the firm.

Bek said hardware companies such as Endurance and Overland still have good reasons to be in Seattle, including testing on Lake Union and in Yakima, access to the sea and University of Washington talent. For Overland, he said, that also means proximity to Joint Base Lewis-McChord.

Combined with the region’s history in aerospace, defense and energy, Winfield noted, those advantages make Seattle one of the country’s key hubs for building physical products. He called it “El Segundo Northwest,” a reference to the Los Angeles-area city that has become a hub for defense and hardware startups.

As Winfield notes, Ascend isn’t the only early-stage firm chasing these companies. Pre-seed firms such as Cantos, Ironspring and Silent Ventures are backing similar founders, and some Seattle firms that historically focused on software are becoming more open to investing in hardware.

Ultimately, he said, Seattle still benefits from a long legacy of technical founders, and Ascend is “betting that those folks are going to continue to create companies that are worth hundreds of billions of dollars.”

“Free Lyric” Tells the Story of a Young Black Female Rapper Cancelled for Her “Provocative” Lyrics

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Watch the new documentary by BAFTA award-winning filmmaker Cherish ‘Chez’ Oteka on Field of Vision.

Free Lyric revisits one of rap culture’s first-ever viral moments: the public shaming of a 17-year-old Black female rapper. Created by BAFTA award-winning filmmaker Cherish ‘Chez’ Oteka, the documentary is now available to watch for free on Field of Vision.

In 2011, O.G. Niki released a freestyle detailing her experience of sleeping with six men on one occasion, a sexual confession that shocked the internet. With the rise of social media as the backdrop, Free Lyric follows the journey of Niki and her unique experience of being an early victim of modern-day cancel culture.

Told as an unfolding narrative during the digital era of the early 2010s, this 31-minute short documentary reconstructs the investigations of internet sleuths and online trolls who worked to expose the identity of the girl behind the controversy. Archival footage and social media clips recreate the overwhelming noise of going viral overnight, while dramatized scenes and present-day interviews deliberately challenge the audience’s certainty in what is and isn’t true.

Public understanding of consent and coercion has evolved since the freestyle initially went viral. As Niki prepares a musical comeback for the first time in 15 years, Free Lyric revisits the events that made her infamous with a different understanding of the sexual encounter that once defined her online.

Field of Vision is honored to collaborate on the film’s release with me too. International, who have created a viewer guide to assist audiences in processing the various themes explored in the film. me too. International is a convener, thought leader, and organizer in the global movement to end sexual and gender-based violence.

How to pinpoint the GiMATE-powered GIGABYTE AI Gaming laptop for you

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GIGABYTE’s boosted AI gaming laptop lineup is here — and gamers, creators, and power users alike should buckle up. Launched at COMPUTEX 2026 this past summer, the machines ship with the latest evolution of GIGABYTE’s on-device AI assistant, GiMATE, under the hood.

Unlike an external AI agent or chatbot, GiMATE is a unified control hub powered by the company’s exclusive, fine-tuned LLM that's built into the system for seamless, hands-on hardware control. The upshot: GiMATE helps you get more from your laptop across gaming, creation, coding, entertainment, and everyday productivity.

While GIGABYTE's AI gaming laptops are all configurable, portable powerhouses, they do have their differences, and GiMATE features vary by model and configuration. Here’s a roundup of GIGABYTE AI gaming laptops to help you score the perfect one.

If extremes are your thing, the AORUS MASTER 16 GEN 2 will melt your face off. The premium AI gaming laptop pairs a blistering AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX3D processor with up to an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5090 Laptop GPU, dominating the HDR1000 OLED gaming arena, especially if you want high-texture settings, ray tracing, and sky-high refresh rates.

All this flagship-class hardware is packed into a surprisingly slim 19mm chassis, with four Dolby Atmos-powered speakers, and topped with a 16-inch, 240Hz OLED display with a 0.2ms response time that doesn’t flinch during gameplay. WINDFORCE INFINITY EX thermals let you go long and strong, while keeping your cool.

In addition to the full suite of updated GiMATE, this laptop also features Customize Mode, which lets you dial in the GPU power and cooling even more, for sessions that feel seriously personal.  It’s also the only model in this lineup with AI Visual Technology, which offers scenario-based display modes: Cinema Mode for richer color, Racing and FPS modes for sharper visuals in fast-paced play, an RPG mode, and E-Paper Mode for more comfortable reading.

As the first-ever GIGABYTE Microsoft Copilot+ PC, the thin-and-light AERO X16 crushes AI workflows and mid-range gaming. And with over 14 hours of battery life, you can do it all off-plug, without having to hunt for an outlet.

For gamers, up to the latest AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 400 Series processor and an RTX 5070 Laptop GPU with 12GB of VRAM easily tear through your fave AAA titles. Thanks to updated GiMATE Creator quantization and that 12GB of VRAM, the machine also runs larger language models (LLMs) more efficiently. GiMATE Creator now supports Qwen-Image for generating visuals from English or Chinese prompts, and on RTX 50 Series Laptop GPUs it taps NVIDIA NVFP4 quantization to run generative AI models faster. And as a Copilot+ PC, it’s your launchpad for Microsoft’s suite of AI image-generation, translation, and day-to-day productivity tools. The 16-inch, 2560 x 1440 IPS display is a dazzler, and a 165Hz refresh rate easily passes muster for both work and play.

The cherry on top? This AI-loaded creator-gamer hybrid starts at 16.75 millimeters thick and weighs 4.19 pounds. You’ll barely feel it in your pack.

This GiMATE-stacked laptop is an excellent pick for AI gamers and creators who want the high-tier graphics of an RTX 5080 Laptop GPU without the flagship price tag.

Powered by an Intel Core 7 240H processor and high-performance graphics capabilities, the feisty machine easily handles your library of AAA games at high to ultra settings. For creators, the power-packed GPU accelerates your video editing, 3D rendering, and AI workflows across the board, thanks to its NVIDIA Blackwell architecture and dedicated GDDR7 VRAM. And if you code, GiMATE Coder is a local AI assistant that helps you generate, debug, and refactor code in Python, JavaScript, C++, and more, right inside Visual Studio Code. Need to present your renderings or marketing deck to the team? The 16-inch, 165Hz IPS WQXGA 2560 x 1440 display lays flat on a 180-degree hinge, making sharing a snap.

All of this GPU firepower, GiMATE’s AI toolkit, and over 12 hours of battery life are squeezed into a trim 19.45mm chassis you can easily tote around to offsite meetings and coffee shops.

Bonus points for the upgradable memory and storage options that offer growing room as you level up.

A 16-inch 2560 x 1440 IPS display with a 16:10 aspect ratio and a fast, 165Hz refresh rate makes for smooth sword fights and crisp visuals for creative work. Among the bundle of baked-in GiMATE, MUX switching really comes in handy here, letting you flip between dGPU-only mode, which runs everything through the dedicated graphics for max performance, and hybrid mode, which leans on the integrated graphics (iGPU) to stretch battery life up to 12 hours, all with a quick restart instead of a trip into the BIOS.

Here's where you need to go to complete Heart of the Fist in Phantom Blade Zero

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Phantom Blade Zero Heart of the Fist side quest is a very simple one, tasking you with finding the grave of the Iron Fist Master and leaving some incense there for the Iron Fiend. Unfortunately, the only hint that the game gives you is that the grave is in the Mirrorwood. Thankfully, it's not very far at all and you will have walked past the grave at least once or twice while playing through Phantom Blade, so this is where to find it.

The Iron Fist Master's grave is very close to the Burial Grounds Bell on the eastern side of the Crimson Forest. Fast travel to this location, then head over to the large grave in the middle of this icy clearing.

Interact with this grave and you'll see a cutscene of Soul burning some incense to honor the fallen master. You'll then immediately be rewarded with the Fist Fragment: Shell item which automatically combines with the Fist Fragment: Core you got after fighting the Iron Fiend the first time. They form the Worldbreaker Fist, which is the necessary item for Soul Tempering the Iron Gauntlets Phantom Edge.And that's the end of Heart of the Fist! Now you should carry on exploring the frozen parts of the Crimson Forest. I recommend going back to the Sha-Chi Tower and sparring with the Jade Dancer and Iron Fiend for the 'Use It Against Them' quest if you haven’t already, as this is how you get the Soul Tempering item for the Fiend Saws dual swords. Then you can finish off the Phantom Blade Zero Triple Threat quest by getting to the end of the forest and facing the Phantom Blade Zero Three-Faced Puppet once again.

Hoskinson says he cannot run Cardano like a CEO. Who is accountable for its growth?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

DReps approve spending, while recipients and administrators answer for funded work that ADA holders can trace through public reports.

Cardano founder Charles Hoskinson said around 28 minutes into his Nov. 7 AMA that he did not control Cardano's treasury or consensus and could not negotiate on behalf of all ADA holders. His argument that he cannot act as the ecosystem's CEO puts a practical question back before holders: who answers for the work meant to grow Cardano?

The answer depends on the decision. Delegated representatives, or DReps, approve treasury spending, the Constitutional Committee checks constitutionality, and funded recipients deliver under the terms of their proposals. Named administrators must monitor fund use and ensure deliverables are achieved. Cardano's treasury guidance explicitly separates approval from reporting on payouts and results.

Anyone can propose a treasury withdrawal. It needs DRep and Constitutional Committee approval, must comply with the Constitution and must remain within the applicable Net Change Limit, the cap on how much ADA can leave the treasury during a period.

Those roles answer different questions. DReps decide whether to support funding. The committee's mandate is limited to constitutionality; it must operate transparently and publish its decisions.

The Constitution's treasury standards require proposals to state their purpose, delivery period, costs and refund circumstances, and disclose whether the prospective recipient received treasury ADA in the previous 24 months.

Funding requests must also allocate ADA for periodic independent audits and oversight metrics, and designate administrators responsible for monitoring spending and ensuring delivery. While an administrator holds withdrawn funds before disbursement, they must remain in separate accounts the community can audit, with the Constitution's restrictions on delegation.

Two approval roles authorize funding. Recipients and named administrators carry distinct responsibilities for delivery and oversight.

Intersect's November 6 update reports that AdaStat's October maintenance milestone was signed off November 2.

The November 3 consensus report maps code and prototype work to funded Initiative 4. The November 6 report keeps it at development, not mainnet release.

Follow the funding decision to the promised work, its administrator and the public delivery record.

The charter describes obligations, not verified compliance across all projects. AdaStat acceptance is attributed to Intersect. Leios remains development work in the November 6 report. Neither example measures ecosystem-wide adoption.

Qualitative responsibility map from current rules, with a selectable comparison of dated public reports. Lines show approval dependencies and monitoring duties, not money amounts, voting weight, a payout order or elapsed time. No calculations or forecasts.

The charter describes obligations, not verified compliance across all projects. AdaStat acceptance is attributed to Intersect. Leios remains development work in the November 6 report. Neither example measures ecosystem-wide adoption.

Cardano's accountability guidance adds a practical benchmark: open milestone reports, published spending, delivery of the proposed work and return of unused funds where applicable. These expectations help holders judge performance. The requirement to fund an audit, however, is distinct from evidence that a particular audit has happened; refund conditions also depend on the approved terms.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

A recent example comes from AdaStat, a Cardano explorer. In its Nov. 6 delivery-assurance update, the Cardano organization Intersect reported that AdaStat's October maintenance milestone was signed off on Nov. 2. According to that account, AdaStat reported security, error-handling, recovery and usability work.

Engineering reports offer another view. The consensus team's Nov. 3 update maps Leios implementation and prototype work to Treasury Funding Initiative 4, and maintenance to Initiative 17. It links code changes and releases, giving readers concrete outputs to inspect alongside the funded scope.

The deployment boundary matters. The Nov. 6 development report says the latest Leios changes on the main development branch remain development work rather than a mainnet release.

Hoskinson remains CEO and founder of Input Output Group, according to its leadership page. His AMA presented building applications as his route to bringing people into Cardano. Later, around 2:19 into the broadcast, he distinguished speaking to his audience personally from speaking as Cardano's CEO.

Delivery responsibility is also spreading across organizations. IO announced Nov. 5 that consensus engineering would move to the firm Serokell and developer experience to AlwaysTrue, with engineers accompanying their work. The moves follow separate schedules through early 2027, with public coordination through Intersect.

Holders also retain a governance response: they can register as DReps, delegate their voting stake to a DRep or change that voting delegation. That gives them a way to act on funding decisions and voting records. Growth still has to emerge from the work those decisions support, and the meaningful test is whether promised outputs become usable products and services.
