Multiple Kennedy family members seek to join legal battle against Trump's triumphal arch

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Trump administration is being challenge of a proposed arch that critics say will obstruct historical sightlines.

Nine members of the Kennedy family sought on Tuesday to join litigation opposing President Donald Trump's bid to build a triumphal arch in the nation's capital.

"President Donald J. Trump is attempting to unlawfully erect a massive arch that will permanently cast shadows over and damage historically significant sightlines to the existing gravesites and memorials established in the Kennedy family’s honor at Arlington National Cemetery, as well as to the memorials of hundreds of thousands of fallen American heroes," their complaint asserted.

FEDERAL JUDGE TEMPORARILY BLOCKS CONSTRUCTION OF TRUMP'S ARLINGTON TRIUMPHAL ARCH HOURS AFTER APPROVAL

President Donald Trump holds a model of the arch as he delivers remarks during a ballroom fundraising dinner in the East Room of the White House on Oct. 15, 2025, in Washington, D.C. (Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images)

"The plaintiffs include Rory Kennedy, Maxwell Kennedy, Christopher G. Kennedy, Kerry Kennedy, Courtney Kennedy, the Hon. Kathleen Kennedy Townsend, and the Hon. Joseph P. Kennedy II, children of Sen. Robert F. Kennedy and Ethel Kennedy; the Hon. Joseph P. Kennedy III, a grandchild of Robert and Ethel Kennedy; and Edward M. Kennedy, Jr., son of Sen. Edward M. Kennedy," according to a Democracy Defenders Fund press release.

Rory and Maxwell Kennedy discussed the issue in a statement on the family's behalf.

"This action is about protecting sacred spaces that belong to the American people. Our parents, Robert and Ethel Kennedy, and our uncles, President John F. Kennedy and Senator Edward M. Kennedy, are laid to rest at Arlington National Cemetery. Our uncle Joseph P. Kennedy Jr., who lost his life serving our country in World War II, is also memorialized there. They are among the more than 400,000 veterans and family members buried or honored on this hallowed ground. We believe Donald Trump has violated the laws and procedures established by Congress to protect this most sacred national memorial. In bringing this lawsuit, we stand for those who can not stand for themselves.

LAWYER SAYS 250-FOOT TRUMP ARCH DISRUPTS HISTORIC SIGHTLINES CHERISHED BY VETERANS SEEKING BURIAL AT ARLINGTON

The graves of President John F. Kennedy and his wife, Jacqueline Kennedy Onassis, at Arlington National Cemetery near Washington, D.C. (Robert Alexander/Getty Images)

"Americans from every corner of the country visit Arlington to honor those who served and sacrificed for our nation. A towering arch casting shadows across the graves of our father, mother, and uncles, as well as the graves of so many who have served our country, would also cast a shadow over our democratic principles, undermining the safeguards established by our elected representatives to protect this hallowed ground for generations to come. Our lawsuit asks the administration to respect the sanctity of Arlington National Cemetery, the laws established to protect it, and the rights of all Americans who come here to honor those who have served our country," the family's statement said.

Last week, a federal judge temporarily blocked the Trump administration from breaking ground on the proposed triumphal arch, shortly after a federal planning commission approved the project. U.S. District Judge Tanya Chutkan issued a 14-day temporary restraining order halting construction.

White House spokesman Davis Ingle issued this statement on Wednesday to Fox News Digital: "An activist district court has temporarily blocked the construction of the long-awaited Arch. It is unfortunate to see that some members of the Kennedy family have joined the litigation against the Arch as the project is intended to honor our heroes who rest in Arlington Cemetery. The judiciary should not be abused to politicize a structure designed to unite our nation. We will continue making the case for this beautiful and fitting monument and are confident we will be fully vindicated as this litigation proceeds. We look forward to seeing the completion of this magnificent Arch—a tribute to 250 years of American greatness."

Trump has said the arch would be utilized for military purposes.

TRUMP SAYS NEW ARCH WILL ALSO DEPLOY DRONES, POSITION SNIPERS AND STORE AMMO

A replica of President Trump's planned "Triumphal Arch" and the U.S. Capitol are seen at the Great American State Fair on July 3, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Joe Raedle/Getty Images)

"At the strong request of the United States Military, and for National Security purposes, I have agreed to convert the magnificent Triumphal Arch, planned since the Civil War Era many years ago, at the Receptive Circle adjoining the Arlington Memorial Bridge, into a top grade Military Complex/Triumphal Arch, to house, store, and have the rapid ability to use large numbers of drones, plus Snipers, on both the roof and plaza areas, and additionally have and hold large quantities of sniper ammunition in storage," he said in a Sept. 20 Truth Social post.

Fox News Digital's Michael Sinkewicz contributed to this report.

Alex Nitzberg is a writer for Fox News Digital.

iOS 27.3 adds new setting to bypass website cookie popups (eventually)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple has several big changes coming to Safari in iOS 27.3, iPadOS 27.3, and macOS 27.3. The browser is adding Vertical Tabs, Adaptive Dark Mode, and new Siri AI powers.

But iOS 27.3 has another Safari feature that’s hidden away in Settings: a new ‘Global Privacy Control’ option.

Inside Settings ⇾ Apps ⇾ Safari ⇾ Advanced you’ll find the ‘Use Global Privacy Control’ setting. Apple describes it this way:

Safari can ask websites to not sell or share your data. This may cause some websites to not function properly or work at all.

The beta also links to a support document where users can find more information, but it’s currently a broken link. The document will likely be published on iOS 27.3’s release day.

Will you enable Global Privacy Control in iOS 27.3? Let us know in the comments.

In practice, it will take time for websites to add support, and it requires users to enable the feature in Safari’s Settings.

A Major Avengers Return Was Just Set Up In VisionQuest, But I Have Another Doomsday Question

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Wait, Did Anthony Mackie Just Spoil A Surprise Avengers: Doomsday Character?

Robert Downey Jr. Coming Back For Doomsday Destroyed One Marvel Record (And Paul Bettany Finally Got Asked About It)

I’ve Missed Nebula And The Guardians, But Karen Gillan Is Giving Me MCU Hope

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

One character's future is confirmed, but...

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Major spoilers for the season finale of Marvel’s VisionQuest, “Forever,” lie ahead, so read on at your own discretion.

VisionQuest has come to an end amid the 2026 TV schedule, and I’m pleased to say series creator Terry Matalas and co. have told one of the most compelling stories in the Marvel Cinematic Universe. The story is relatively self-contained, but this is the MCU we’re talking about, which means Easter eggs and major teases are par for the course. On that note, one of the finale's post-credits scenes teed up the return of a powerful character. That aside, though, I have another question regarding Avengers: Doomsday.

If there’s any one character that Marvel fans have been asking for in recent years, it’s Wanda Maximoff a.k.a. the Scarlet Witch. The all-powerful character (played perfectly by Elizabeth Olsen) seemed to meet her demise at the end of 2022’s Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness. At that time, she appeared to have been crushed under Mount Wundagore, and official Marvel media seemed to seal Wanda’s fate. Not only that, but Olsen claimed to not be aware of any plans for her character. However, we now know the truth.

The second post-credits scene of VisionQuest takes viewers back to the moment Wanda is supposedly crushed by that mountain. Ultimately, the rubble does fall on her, but her body is recovered and revived by none other than Doctor Doom, who is the main antagonist of Doomsday. From there, Doom says, “Scarlet Witch, awaken. Your power will be mine.” With that, Wanda’s eyes pop open, solidifying the character’s return to the land of the living.

So it would seem that all those rumors about Scarlet Witch showing up in the aforementioned 2026 movie schedule entry weren’t for naught. This, more specifically, lends credence to the notion of Wanda being crucial to Doom’s multiverse-altering plans. I’m eager to see how she factors into the story but, for now, I have a Doomsday question pertaining to another character.

There’s one other powerful character who’s still in the MCU mix following the events of VisionQuest. I’m talking about the one-and-only Ultron, who’s been impeccably played by James Spader. Following the death of his lover, Jocasta, at the hands of Virgil Horton, the A.I. warlord attempts to wipe out London using the nanobots he’s been merged with. Vision ultimately convinces him otherwise and, while it initially appears that Ultron dies after destroying the nanobots, he saves some in order to retain his existence.

Ultron subsequently decides to live amongst humanity and, as revealed in the first post-credits scene, he still has Jocasta's data drive. Of course, it’s fair to wonder whether he’ll put that to use, but what I’m really wondering is whether Ultron will return in either Doomsday or its sequel, Avengers: Secret Wars.

Now that the sentient being has some level of appreciation for humanity and the world, I can’t help but think he’d take issue with Doom’s machinations. I’m not so sure he’d directly ally himself with Earth’s Mightiest Heroes, but could the newly nanobot-infused being still intervene in the conflict in a major way?

I’m not sure what lies ahead for Ultron, but I’m curious as to what may or may not happen with his story. Meanwhile, I’m just glad to get some confirmation regarding Scarlet Witch’s long-ambiguous fate. Keep an eye out for her in Avengers: Doomsday, which opens in theaters on December 18. And, of course, stream all of VisionQuest using a Disney+ subscription.

Entertainment 360 Buys Influencer Firm Brigade As It Expands In Creator Economy

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The talent management firm's acquisition of the Max Stein-run boutique adds a client roster of Substackers and podcast players.

Entertainment 360 has accelerated its drive into the creator economy by acquiring Brigade, the creative management firm focused on digital talent.

Terms of the deal were not disclosed, but the acquisition brings elite digital tastemakers, including Substack writers, podcasters and influencers, to the Los Angeles-based talent management company run by a collective of partners as it moves beyond traditional film and TV representation.

Brigade founder Max Stein will become a partner at Entertainment 360 and bring his team and clients with him to help the combined company tap a growing influencer universe and manage and monetize celebrity creators coveted by brand marketers.

“The ways audiences discover and engage with entertainment have changed, and so have the needs of the talent building careers today,” the Entertainment 360 partners said in a statement. “Max recognized this evolution early and he and his exceptional team have built a remarkable business representing exactly the kind of original, forward-thinking talent we have always sought to work with. Bringing Brigade into 360 expands what we can offer our clients and strengthens our ability to create extraordinary opportunities for artists across an increasingly diverse entertainment landscape.”

Entertainment 360 will look to Brigade to help sign digital talent fostered by Stein and others to brand deals and partnerships, and new business opportunities in publishing, podcasts and traditional media.

“I’ve always been drawn to people with a singular point of view, whose work and ambitions don’t fit neatly into one category,” said Stein in his own statement. “Our clients have built remarkable careers by following their own taste and instincts, and our job has been to help them turn those ideas into lasting businesses. Entertainment 360 shares that philosophy and brings an extraordinary depth of experience, relationships, and resources. Joining forces gives our clients access to many more opportunities, while allowing us to preserve the personal approach that has always defined Brigade.”

The Brigade acquisition comes as YouTube stars, podcasters and other digital creators increasingly rise to the fore in Hollywood and a fast-shifting entertainment ecosystem. Brigade’s clients include fashion writer Leandra Medine Cohen; Feed Me newsletter writer Emily Sundberg; Chris Black and Jason Stewart, How Long Gone podcast hosts; stylist Danielle Goldberg; Karley Sciortino, the I Want Your Sex writer and producer; TV personality Zanna Roberts Rassi; and After School newsletter founder Casey Lewis.

The acquisition follows Entertainment 360’s recent hires and expanding into lifestyle and digital business representation in an evolving entertainment industry.

American Airlines Plans To Bring Back First Class Mini-Meals On Shorter Flights—Starting With New York–Chicago

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This is a very good change. A flight can take up much of your afternoon without crossing American’s 900-mile meal threshold, especially when you include getting to the airport, boarding, and a connection. A small meal makes first class more worthwhile.

JonNYC reports that American Airlines is bringing back light bites and mini-meals on 700–899-mile domestic first class flights, starting on NYC/ORD.

American used to have a service between a basket of packaged snacks and a full meal. Before the pandemic, shorter flights could get something plated that would tide you over.

In 2016 I reported plans for plated morning items, including yogurt and breakfast cake. And my 2018 menu coverage included Lite Bites cheeses with cracked black pepper cheddar, cave-aged cheddar, Appalachian Tomme, and smoked Gouda.

American’s mainline standard is a snack basket on 500–899-mile flights during meal times, with meals starting in the 900-mile range.

United actually raised its meal threshold from 800 to 900 miles in January 2024. American would be moving in the other direction, offering something more substantial on flights where Delta and United generally hand out snacks.

Alaska is still the standard to beat. They already feed passengers on shorter flights, and I’ve found their domestic first class food better than competitors’.

New York–Chicago is an obvious place to start. New York LaGuardia–O’Hare is about 730 miles, and New York JFK–O’Hare about 740. An expansion might include:

American also used to have meal “exception markets”: shorter routes that got meals for competitive reasons. My January 2020 service guide listed Chicago–Dallas, Chicago–Boston, Chicago–New York, Los Angeles–Denver, and LaGuardia–Atlanta, among others.

I like a broad 700–899-mile rule better than making customers depend on whether their particular city pair merits an exception, though the exception markets got real meals and some were even shorter like Chicago–Washington National at just 612 miles which still wouldn’ qualify for a lite bite under this distance band.

This does mean more work for flight attendants. Individual food service means preparation, distribution, and clearing, with less time to do it on a shorter flight. American is also adding first class seats to some of its aircraft.

The airline’s product turnaround has been surprising, and surprisingly fast. They’ve introduced new business class suites, improved regional cabins and internet, upgraded coffee, and put money into lounges. They’re also reversing years of bad product decisions, including plans to bring back seatback screens and add more extra legroom coach seats.

Their returning coach food reservation program, announced with an initial Dallas–Fort Worth–Los Angeles test, should help passengers get the item they actually want. Mostly, though, I like that American seems to be loading enough food that there’s still something left when the cart reaches my aisle.

American has now announced the return of lite bites, with options including:

There will be two options onboard a given flight, with choices rotating “throughout the year.”

Mortgage Demand Jumps 6.2% as Rates Fall For Fourth Straight Week

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mortgage applications rose last week, as interest rates decreased for the fourth week in a row, hitting their lowest levels since mid-September, as a positive jobs report and the results of the midterm election bolster the outlook.

For the week ending Nov. 6 the Mortgage Bankers Association's Market Composite Index—a measure of total mortgage loan application volume—increased 6.2% on a seasonally adjusted basis from one week earlier.

The Purchase Index, which measures the volume of mortgage applications to purchase a single-family home, increased 8% from one week earlier after seasonal adjustment. Purchase applications were down 18% from a year ago.

According to MBA calculations, the average contract interest rate for 30-year fixed mortgages with conforming loan balances is 7.21%, a slight decrease from last week's rate of 7.3%. Rates averaged 5.99% a year ago.

Mortgage rates dropped to 7.18% last week from 7.21% the week before, according to a separate survey from Freddie Mac.

“Mortgage rates declined for the fourth straight week,” said Joel Kan, MBA’s vice president and deputy chief economist. “The declining trend in rates helped to spur stronger application activity, although the pace of purchase and refinance applications remain considerably lower than a year ago."

In recent weeks, high interest rates had pushed more buyers toward riskier Adjustable Rate Mortgages. However, this past week, ARM applications decreased to 10.9% of the total. applications, down from 11.4% the previous week.

FHA and VA loans saw "particularly strong growth" this past week, said Kan.

The share of FHA loan applications decreased to 15.5% from 16% the prior week. The VA share of total applications increased to 12% from 11.2% the prior week. And the USDA share of total applications decreased to 0.5% from 0.6% the week prior.

The refinance share of mortgage activity remained unchanged at 39.8%, but was 57% lower than the same week one year ago, as homeowners remain locked into their lower interest rates.

October's positive jobs report, which showed that the U.S. economy added 165,000 nonfarm jobs in October, has increased the odds that the Federal Reserve will raise interest rates when it next meets in December.

CME Group's FedWatch has the likelihood of an additional rate hike at around 77%.

The positive jobs report news was compounded by broad Democrat wins in the midterm elections. Democrats, many of whom ran on a message of affordability, won the majority in both the House and the Senate. Voters aged 18 to 34 and men aged 18 to 49 had ranked housing costs as a top political issue in a rcent CNBC survey.

Trump's former Federal Housing Commissioner Frank Cassidy called the midterms a "wake-up call."

"Everyone needs to get serious about tackling and addressing housing affordability. It can't be ignored anymore," he told Realtor.com®.

Mortgage rates are calculated based on various factors in the economy, and the length of your loan and credit score will also factor into the mortgage rate you qualify for.

the 10-year Treasury note, because most 30-year mortgages are either paid off or refinanced in roughly eight to 11 years.

That makes the duration on the loans roughly comparable, and mortgage lenders use the 10-year Treasury as a benchmark for setting rates, adding on a risk premium.

Long-term yields for Treasury notes are determined by a number of factors, including the supply of and demand for U.S. government debt, and investor expectations for inflation over the life of the bonds.

SpaceX develops its own ‘Kessel’ rack-scale system featuring 72 Nvidia Rubin GPUs for data centers in space, report claims

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SpaceX/xAI is developing a custom version of Nvidia's Vera Rubin NVL72 rack-scale AI system that replaces conventional NVLink cable assemblies with a massive 84-layer printed circuit board (PCB), in part for use in orbital data centers, reports SemiAnalysis. The system is codenamed Kessel and retains 36 Vera CPUs, 72 Rubin GPUs, and nine NVLink switch trays, but introduces a different solid-state interconnect architecture. While SpaceX/xAI will continue to buy Nvidia hardware directly from the company, Elon Musk's company will source other components independently, which could give it greater control over its hardware supply chain.

The Kessel system retains the fundamental configuration of Nvidia's Vera Rubin NVL72 platform, which comprises 72 GPU packages distributed across 18 compute trays and nine NVLink switch trays. However, instead of Nvidia's conventional arrangement with internal NVLink cable cartridges, SpaceX plans to position compute and switch trays back-to-back around a passive PCB midplane. The architecture resembles a smaller version of Nvidia's Kyber design with 576 AI GPUs (albeit in 144 packages), which also relies on a PCB-based interconnect rather than conventional cable assemblies, SemiAnalysis notes. Interestingly, the design is aimed at both terrestrial and orbital deployments, according to the analysts.

Arguably the most distinctive feature of Kessel is its 84-layer PCB midplane, which uses ultra-low-loss M10 copper-clad laminates (CCLs). By contrast, Nvidia's Kyber architecture relied on a backplane based on a considerably more complex 156-layer PCB featuring M9 CCLs. The reduction in layer count by nearly half could substantially improve manufacturability and production yields, but the more advanced copper-clad laminate will almost certainly increase material costs. In any case, an 84-layer PCB is a device that is exceptionally hard to build.

The move to a PCB midplane could simplify system assembly by eliminating numerous high-speed cable connections, but it would not necessarily reduce manufacturing costs. An 84-layer PCB that relies on ultra-low-loss M10 laminate is an exceptionally expensive component that requires sophisticated manufacturing processes and precise quality control. To put an 84-layer PCB number into context, modern high-end server motherboards use 15 – 20 layers, whereas server development boards use more than 20 layers to virtually eliminate any electrical issues with the board itself. Furthermore, manufacturing yields of an 84-layer PCB could become a major obstacle, as defects in internal layers may render an entire board unusable. Consequently, the economic viability of Kessel will depend on whether savings from cable elimination and independent component procurement can offset the cost of its complex midplane.

Perhaps more importantly, Kessel represents a departure from Nvidia's conventional system-level procurement model. Instead of purchasing Nvidia's assembled L6 compute boards or even L10 trays that Nvidia plans to sell with its Vera Rubin generation, SpaceX reportedly plans to acquire AI accelerators, CPUs, switches, and networking components directly from Nvidia and procure other components from third-party suppliers (one can wonder how easy it will be for SpaceX/xAI to procure memory amid today's shortages). This would enable SpaceX to select its own PCB manufacturers, component vendors, and manufacturing partners, optimize its hardware for its infrastructure (which will be important for orbital deployments), and perhaps reduce intermediary costs. Meanwhile, the company would assume greater responsibility for system design, validation, manufacturing quality, and supply-chain management, which is something that most hyperscalers have been doing in recent years.

For Nvidia, the implications are less straightforward. Although direct sales of CPU and AI GPU packages could reduce the company's revenue from complete compute-board assemblies, the arrangement could also encourage other large customers to develop their own Nvidia-based systems and eventually deploy more Nvidia-based machines, something that may be important in a world where custom silicon developed by hyperscalers is rapidly gaining traction. However, to make this happen, Kessel must be a smashing success that will offer clear advantages over systems designed by Nvidia.

Siri co-founder backs Helix for its voice ID tech

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For years, websites have relied on technology like CAPTCHA to prove that the user on the other end is human. More recently, social media platforms have turned to photos or videos to verify users’ identities. Now, a startup called Helix is betting that voice technology can do the same job, without requiring users to hand over sensitive personal information.

The voice technology, which can run directly on a user’s device, has attracted backing from Siri co-founder Dag Kittlaus, who will also act as an advisor.

The company raised $6 million in funding in a round led by Chicago Ventures, with participation from BAM Ventures, Aglaé Ventures, Corazon Capital, Tusk Ventures, Yonder Ventures, Rule30, and Brown Alumni Group.

The startup was founded by Alex Oberg, Anna Dorofiyenko, and Mike Hall in 2025. Oberg previously worked at Ticketmaster, which acquired his startup; Dorofiyenko led data science teams at Live Nation and Zillow; and Hall has worked at Nike and Tinder, where he helped develop the initial matching algorithm.

At Ticketmaster, Oberg played a role in creating Verified Fan, which is designed to keep bots out of the platform. As AI advanced, he started thinking along the same lines for a new solution.

“As AI started to come up in the last two years, I was realizing that it’s going to make it increasingly difficult to determine who’s human and who’s not on the Internet. Identity on the Internet’s always been about proxies for identity, right? It’s your email, it’s your phone, it’s your IP address, it’s your device fingerprint, it’s your device ID,” Oberg said.

He noted that with the rise of AI agents like Instinct and Muse, the industry needs to rethink human identity on the internet and rely on biometric signals like voice, which is where Helix comes in.

Its core technology can run directly on a device to determine whether someone is human and estimate their age. The company is also running pilots to explore whether voice can be used a biomarker to detect conditions such as adrunkenness or distress.

The company can deploy its technology to replace or supplement CAPTCHA and help block bot attacks. Helix said that because its model can run locally even on an iPhone 13, the cost of deploying the technology is minimal. The startup said one of its customers is Zeel, an in-home healthcare provider that uses Helix’s tech to verify the identities of providers visiting customers’ homes.

Oberg said verifying workers, such as cab drivers, is another use case the startup is exploring. He also said that age-verification regulations for social media platforms around the world are forcing users to trade privacy for access. Helix’s voice technology, he said, could offer an alternative.

“There’s like an avalanche of regulation coming down because essentially society has decided children should not be able to use the Internet as adults. That has created a bit of a strawman argument that everyone needs to trade their privacy for the sake of security and child safety. We are that company that’s saying, nope, don’t agree. It’s a four-second voice challenge,” he said.

The CEO also emphasized the limitations of Helix’s technology, noting that it cannot reliably detect whether someone is lying. He added that its voice technology doesn’t identify individual speakers and claimed that even if the company stores a voiceprint, it cannot be used to reconstruct a person’s voice.

AI-powered fraud is also a growing problem in phone calls. But Helix believes its technology isn’t yet capable of reliably detecting AI agents during live calls.

Siri co-founder Kittlaus said that voice identification technology has finally reached a point where it works reliably, positioning Helix to scale as AI-powered fraud increases. He also said that Helix’s work to compress its models gives the startup a cost advantage.

“What folks at Helix are doing is, instead of teaching computers how to understand speech, these guys are understanding, what the speech of the speaker actually means, what’s going on with them, who they are they lying, are they impaired? That side of it is fascinating to me within the larger picture that I just described,” he told TechCrunch.

Visibility Gets You On The List, Evidence Helps You Stay There via @sejournal, @billhunt

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

AI visibility can hide evidence gaps that weaken buyer recommendations as requirements get specific, making decision readiness a crucial GEO metric.

For much of the past two years, the AI search conversation has focused on visibility. Teams want to know whether their brand is mentioned, where it appears in recommendations, how often competitors surface, and whether they are being cited across ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Perplexity, Google, and other AI surfaces.

Those are useful metrics, but I have increasingly questioned whether they tell us enough about the corresponding business outcome.

Nearly two years ago, I began using the term “eligibility gates” to describe the detailed decision criteria that determine whether a company or product remains qualified as a buyer moves through the decision process. I later expanded on that idea when I wrote Decision Coverage: whether an organization has exposed enough information and evidence for AI to evaluate, compare, qualify, and confidently recommend.

That led to two related arguments tied to previous articles. In my article Beyond Content Parity, I argued that using tools and processes that simply match competitor content may create parity without creating any decision advantage. In Beyond Brand Sovereignty, I argued that the knowledge AI needs often exists beyond the marketing site, scattered across product, sales, support, engineering, legal, customer success, and external sources.

A recent project allowed me to test those ideas against an actual business problem.

The company did not have an AI visibility problem. It frequently appeared in AI-generated consideration sets. The concern was what happened afterward, when prospective buyers continued the conversation and began asking more specific questions about requirements, workflows, integrations, outcomes, evidence, and competitive differences.

The question was not “How do we get seen in AI?” It was, “Why, with all this visibility, is our lead pipeline shrinking?” The reality we found was that as AI took a prospective buyer further down the consideration set, they became less competitive.

To prove my thesis and identify the problem, we created 38 detailed, criteria-based prompts across several product categories and buyer segments, and evaluated them on five AI surfaces, yielding 190 responses.

These were intentionally different from broad “best vendor” prompts. They reflected the kinds of requirements buyers use to narrow a field: workflow fit, integrations, regulatory requirements, interoperability, operational conditions, analytics, platform breadth, and other decision criteria. I had to create a detailed explanation of their purpose as diagnostic-only because the brand and their SEO agency argued that no human would ever use that complex a prompt. We actually found that some prompts were even longer after talking to actual procurement managers.

The initial numbers looked excellent – even better than their current visibility scorecard. Our research found the company was mentioned in 98.4% of evaluated responses and appeared as either a primary recommendation or shortlisted option 93.2% of the time. For many GEO programs, that would be the headline of the week and champagne for everyone. The brand was almost always visible and almost always treated as a serious option. But visibility was not the problem we were investigating.

When we evaluated those same responses against the decision criteria in the prompts, 73.7% had weak or missing evidence supporting important claims or recommendations about the company. In 15.8% of runs, an important association, decision criterion, competitive distinction, or discovery signal contained what we classified as a Significant or Critical Gap. The more we refined our evidence-scoring criteria, the higher this number rose.

The critical finding was that AI systems generally knew the company belonged in the conversation. However, they were far less consistent at substantiating why it should remain there once the buyer began applying specific eligibility criteria. That is the distinction that matters and explains their declining lead pipeline.

There is a meaningful difference between asking which vendors operate in a market and asking which vendors can satisfy a specific combination of technical, operational, regulatory, and business requirements.

A model may know a company is a legitimate vendor, understand its main products, and have enough general information to put it on a shortlist. But once the buyer asks how an integration works, whether a workflow is supported, what happens under a particular operating condition, what limitations apply, or what evidence supports an outcome claim, the information requirements become more demanding. Those are not merely additional keywords. They are evidence requirements.

That explains how a brand can achieve a 98.4% mention rate and 93.2% primary-or-shortlist rate while still having weak or missing evidence in nearly three-quarters of the responses. The numbers are not contradictory. They measure different stages of the decision process.

No one can dispute that AI visibility gets the company into the consideration set. Decision readiness determines whether AI has enough information to keep it there.

See also: B2B Buyers Choose A Vendor Before They Reach Out – 3 Ways To Be Visible When It Counts

One of the more important findings was that weak evidence did not necessarily mean the company lacked the capability. In many cases, the capability appeared to exist, but the available information did not explain or substantiate it well enough to satisfy the AI surface’s confidence level.

A marketing bullet might claim an end-to-end process without ever showing the actual process, connections, or how the data flows between systems. We found efficiency claims but did not have any “proof” from internal tests or customer testimonials of the efficiency gain.

Sometimes the information was simply fragmented. A model might need to connect a product page to an integration page, then to another interoperability page, before it could infer how the pieces satisfied the buyer’s requirement. While an AI system could or should be able to make those multi-part connections, each additional inference creates another opportunity for incomplete representation or for a competitor with clearer evidence to become the safer recommendation.

We also found that the strongest content was not necessarily the longest or most buzzword-integrated, but rather content that made the relationships among capability, workflow, buyer requirements, and outcome explicit.

An interesting finding was that only first-party evidence was not inherently a weakness. Many claims lacked third-party validation, but this was not necessarily flagged as a defect. We found that authoritative product documentation may be the best evidence that a feature or integration exists. However, every claim involving superiority, effectiveness, adoption, scale, measurable outcomes, or competitive differentiation did benefit from independent corroboration by customers, industry, government, analysts, or other parties.

That means some evidence gaps are not content-writing problems at all, but does anyone else agree with the evidence?

See also: Schema, LLMs & The Low Bar For ‘Evidence’ In GEO

The findings reinforced the concern I raised in my earlier work about how easy-button AI content-generation systems create “me-too content parity.” As I explained to this brand, most of the GEO efforts to date were what I would call SEO-adjacent GEO. It is a newly rebranded SEO process that leverages AI speed for traditional content-gap analysis. Teams look at competitor coverage, People Also Ask questions, common prompts, and pages receiving citations, then create content to close those gaps. There is nothing inherently wrong with that. In this case, it appears to have worked extremely well at establishing this company’s category presence. The limitation appears when everyone says roughly the same thing, and you don’t offer details.

If every vendor claims to be end-to-end, highly integrated, AI-enabled, scalable, flexible, and focused on efficiency, those statements may help establish that each belongs in the category. They do much less when the model has to determine what “end-to-end” covers, what “integration” connects, what the AI actually does, or what evidence supports the efficiency claim.

Parity can help a company qualify for the conversation without providing enough information to survive deeper evaluation.

See also: AI Gives You The Vocabulary. It Doesn’t Give You The Expertise

Running the prompts was relatively straightforward. There wasn’t a tool or off-the-shelf framework for what we were trying to measure. We needed to build a multi-variable evidence scorecard capable of determining whether the model had enough support to evaluate the company against the criteria that actually mattered in the buying decision.

That meant defining what counted as strong evidence, what first-party documentation was enough, and when independent corroboration became important. The bigger challenge was thinking beyond the “we are great” organizational mindset to determine when an evidence gap or omission was serious enough to affect consideration or selection. That turned out to be even harder than getting SEOs to agree on ranking factors.

Those judgments then had to be translated into a repeatable scoring system and applied consistently across the prompts and five AI surfaces. It also required conversations beyond content. Sales had to help identify criteria and objections that affect deals. Product had to validate capabilities and limitations. Customer-facing teams helped identify real outcomes. In some cases, customers themselves were the best source for understanding what evidence mattered.

This is why I do not believe there will be one universal GEO evidence scorecard that every organization can download. The methodology can be reusable if you can honestly define the decision criteria and what constitutes sufficient evidence, then evaluate whether AI can connect the brand to those criteria.

As we evaluated eligibility criteria and evidence requirements, many findings no longer looked like conventional content gaps. Looking at this massive evidence gap matrix as one manager exclaimed, “that no human will ever read,” it was obvious this would become a team effort to make the changes. Content teams cannot make up content, nor will they necessarily know how everything works.

Some of the knowledge required to close these gaps is organizational in nature. We had to pry it out of product, engineering, and sales, and then run it through marketing and compliance. In some cases, we had to loop in external and partner relations when stronger third-party or attributable proof was required beyond what we could create.

The content team still has a critical role: organize that knowledge, make relationships explicit, create coherent evidence paths, and expose information in ways humans and AI systems can understand.

Most importantly, this project validated and reinforced my belief that measuring only the first AI response is insufficient. True, those broad PAA-like prompts are valuable for generating a list of options worth considering. The problem is the current tools cannot maintain state like a human workflow and aggregate the sequence of prompt turns most users leverage. Each subsequent prompt digs deeper into the criteria for consideration, maintaining preferences, and memory, and culminating in the multivariate result that I affectionately call prompt math.

This means that GEO measurement needs to become more sophisticated and account for both visibility and decision readiness. Many tools now have answer accuracy monitoring, which is a step forward. I argue that we need to put more effort into the element that is much closer to the business problem organizations should actually care about.

There is an obvious danger here because once marketers conclude that AI systems need evidence, we will probably see an explosion of “evidence-shaped content”: longer pages, superficial diagrams, weak surveys, synthetic research, and AI-generated case studies created primarily because someone believes those formats will influence an LLM, just like the explosion of Reddit submission services. That misses the point. The objective is not to manufacture artifacts that appear persuasive to AI, but to provide real content that reduces uncertainty and risk for the buyer.

If the information would not help a knowledgeable customer make a better decision, it probably should not exist merely to improve a GEO score.

As noted, the company had already achieved what many GEO programs would consider an amazing success: 98.4% visibility and 93.2% primary-or-shortlist consideration, yet 73.7% of those same responses had weak or missing evidence. Unfortunately, our analysis could not prove that this evidence gap caused every lost sale or every subsequent AI exclusion. What it does show is that a visibility-only measurement model would have missed the weakness we were asked to investigate.

Any company can succeed in entering the conversation but fail to offer enough credible evidence to remain qualified as the conversation becomes more specific.

Why Pronostyx Is Building Prediction Markets for Questions the Big Platforms Ignore

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Global prediction markets mostly list the same English-language questions. Pronostyx takes a different approach: local, niche markets for Spanish-speaking audiences, settled on-chain against named public sources.

Illustrative Spanish-language markets on Pronostyx, each with its named resolution source. Image: Pronostyx.

Prediction markets have had a breakout few years. What began as a niche corner of crypto has become a mainstream way to put a number on uncertain events, from elections and central bank decisions to championship finals. Newsrooms now quote market odds next to polls, and trading volumes have grown accordingly.

Yet for all that growth, the product on offer is remarkably uniform. The largest platforms list broadly the same questions, written in English, framed around US and global headlines. For hundreds of millions of people the events they actually follow day to day are barely represented.

Pronostyx was built to cater to all of those people.

Pronostyx is a prediction market platform focused on niche markets: specific, local questions that large global venues tend to overlook. Instead of competing on the same handful of headline events, it creates markets around the topics that dominate conversation in particular communities. Which creates more engagement and a closer link to the platform.

That starts with the Spanish-speaking market. Spanish is spoken natively by close to 500 million people across Spain, Latin America and the United States, but prediction markets in the language are almost non-existent. Pronostyx is Spanish-first by design. Markets are written natively in Spanish, not machine-translated. Questions reflect local politics, local football leagues, economic data releases and cultural events. The platform is also fully bilingual, with an English interface for users who prefer it.

The thesis is simple. People trade on what they know. A user in Madrid or Mexico City has a real information edge on a question about their own league or their own parliament, and almost no edge on a question about a distant election. Niche markets reward that local knowledge, and they produce price signals that do not exist anywhere else.

Pronostyx combines its own technology with established on-chain infrastructure, to offer the best liquidity and a large amount of markets.

Hybrid liquidity. Markets on Pronostyx draw on two sources: the platform's own liquidity and liquidity from external venues. This gives users depth on widely traded questions while allowing Pronostyx to list local markets that external venues do not carry.

Automated pricing. For its own markets, Pronostyx uses automated market maker (AMM) pricing. An AMM keeps a market tradeable even when few participants are active, which matters for niche questions where a traditional order book would sit empty.

On-chain settlement. Trades are executed and settled on-chain on Base, an Ethereum layer-2 network. Settlement rules are transparent and verifiable rather than dependent on an internal ledger.

Named resolution sources. Every market states, before trading opens, the exact public source that will decide the outcome, and that source is locked. For Spanish markets this means official bodies such as the Boletín Oficial del Estado (BOE) or Spain's national statistics institute (INE). Users know in advance how a market will be settled, which removes one of the most common sources of disputes.

Crypto and card access. Users can fund their accounts with crypto wallets, standard payment cards as well as local payment options. That lowers the barrier for users who are new to digital assets, while keeping settlement on-chain.

How a trade moves on Pronostyx, from deposit to on-chain settlement and resolution against a named public source. Image: Pronostyx.

The value of a prediction market lies in the information its price carries. A market on a question nobody else covers is not a smaller version of a big market. It is a new data point. Local markets can aggregate knowledge that polls, analysts and global platforms miss, because they invite the people closest to an event to express a view.

Niche markets also broaden who participates. Many Spanish speakers have never used a prediction market, not because the concept does not interest them, but because nothing was built for them. Native language, familiar topics and simple funding options turn a crypto-native product into something a much wider audience can use.

How Pronostyx's niche-market approach differs from a typical global prediction market. Image: Pronostyx.

For the crypto ecosystem, that means more on-chain activity coming from users and regions that are currently underrepresented.

The Pronostyx web platform has been live since June 2026 and currently lists hundreds of active markets across politics, sports, economics, crypto and culture. A mobile app is next on the roadmap, starting with Android.

The team plans to keep expanding its catalogue of local markets in Spanish-speaking countries, then apply the same niche-market model to other European markets underserved by the large platforms.

Pronostyx does not offer investment advice, and trading on prediction markets carries the risk of losing the full amount staked. Availability depends on local regulations, and users are responsible for checking the rules that apply where they live.

Explore niche, Spanish-language prediction markets on politics, sports and the economy, settled on-chain against named public sources.

Launch editorial-quality partner coverage with a built-in CTA designed to turn attention into qualified traffic.

Hubble Space Telescope reveals glittering stars with mysterious origins | Space photo of the day for Nov. 11, 2026

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

This dazzling collection of ancient stars could hold clues to the Milky Way's past.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Hubble Space Telescope has captured a breathtaking view of thousands of stars glittering like cosmic jewels in a dazzling display of blue, gold and orange.

But this spectacular scene is far more than just a pretty picture. Some of these stars are almost as old as the universe itself and could hold clues to how our Milky Way formed.

The exquisite image released by ESA on Nov. 5 shows Messier 30 (M30), a densely packed collection of stars located approximately 27,500 light-years from Earth in the constellation Capricornus.

M30 is a globular cluster, a roughly spherical collection of tens of thousands to millions of stars held together by gravity.

Our Milky Way is home to at least 150 of these ancient stellar gatherings, some of which are estimated to be between 11.5 billion and 13.5 billion years old. For comparison, the universe itself is approximately 13.8 billion years old!

Besides looking like a vast cosmic treasure trove glittering across the universe, M30 also has a fascinating story to tell.

Globular clusters are essentially cosmic time capsules. Their ancient stars preserve chemical clues about the conditions that existed when they formed, helping astronomers piece together the Milky Way's early history.

Astronomers have also discovered that M30, along with several other globular clusters, traverses the Milky Way in the opposite direction to the galaxy's rotation. This unusual motion raises the intriguing possibility that M30 may not have formed in our galaxy at all. It could have simply been captured by the Milky Way billions of years ago.

Daisy Dobrijevic joined Space.com in February 2022, having previously worked as a staff writer for All About Space magazine. She completed an editorial internship with BBC Sky at Night Magazine and worked at the National Space Centre, communicating space science to the public.

Daisy holds a PhD in plant physiology and a Master's in Environmental Science. Based in Nottingham, U.K., she covers all things space, with a special focus on solar activity and space weather. She also has a keen interest in astrotourism and is always on the lookout for the next northern lights adventure.

She will join Hurtigruten as an onboard astronomer for a northern lights sailing in January 2027.

World of Warcraft: Forever review: "A love letter to the MMORPG I fell for over 20 years ago"

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Get the biggest gaming news, reviews, and releases straight to your inbox.

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

World of Warcraft: Forever is a love letter to why we all fell for it in 2005. It still has a familiar charm but it adds on extra areas, extra quests, and a plethora of fantastic user interface improvements which makes it feel far more modern than before. It's everything that veteran players have longed for.

A beautiful reinvention of a much-loved MMO

User interface changes and tweaks are improvements

It's still World of Warcraft at heart, so it won't convert naysayers

Why you can trust GamesRadar+ Our experts review games, movies and tech over countless hours, so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about our reviews policy.

As I created a Hunter in World of Warcraft: Forever for what must be the 100th time by now, the saying 'the definition of insanity is doing the same thing over and over again and expecting different results' rolled around my head a lot. I've played this game before. A lot. Is Forever truly sufficiently different to be so obsessed again? Well, yes. It's this lovely, warm, flawed, wonderfully infuriating dose of nostalgia. It's simultaneously far improved from the original World of Warcraft and, more recently, World of Warcraft Classic (we'll get into the differences shortly), while leaving you with questions of 'why would you leave that in?'.

Will it convert those who never liked WoW in the first place? Of course not. I don't think that's the point of World of Warcraft: Forever. Instead, it brings home all those slightly lost souls from 20+ years ago (AKA millennials like me who want to return to familiar comfort once more), but with some core improvements. It does that incredibly well and given it has brought back a record number of lapsed players, so far it's succeeding.

I've been playing World of Warcraft since the closed beta days in 2004. I've reviewed many World of Warcraft expansion packs (including my WoW: Midnight review this year), played Classic substantially, and have now skipped meals and sleep in favor of playing Forever. I've played seemingly every other mainstream MMO you can think of too, and should probably learn to step away from the PC more often but the allure of WoW remains too strong. – Reviewer Jen Allen

Release date: November 4, 2026Platform(s): PCDeveloper: In-housePublisher: Blizzard Entertainment

Before I dive too deep, let's sum up the differences between World of Warcraft, World of Warcraft Classic, and World of Warcraft Forever so you actually know why the game's important. Retail is best seen as an entirely different beast to Forever. It's very fast-paced, throws nearly as many unlocks at you as a standard game of Fortnite, and is all about leveling up as fast as possible to get to the next part of the story. Getting from level 1 to the level cap of 80 can take just 10 hours if you know what you're doing. It has its place for sure and I really like it, but it's far removed from World of Warcraft's launch years, which is where Classic comes in.

Classic took the game right back to the start and has gradually introduced expansion packs in chronological order. It has a linear progression path, while its leveling process is slow-paced. Each level is richly earned and you'll spend around 200 hours or more playing to reach the level cap of 70. Crucially, not much has been done to spruce anything up. There are some odd UI choices, it looks increasingly dated, and it feels old. It's familiar and fun but also fairly rough around the edges once you start looking.

World of Warcraft: Forever takes what many loved about Classic, but with some key quality-of-life improvements. Player level is permanently capped at 60 with horizontal rather than vertical progression. Oh, and there's a new race called the Skyborne and a bunch of new race/class combinations. You'd be forgiven for thinking Forever still sounds a lot like Classic from this brief overview. It kind of is. But, it's better. This feels like a take on World of Warcraft Classic fans like me wanted, truer to the original experience but willing to remix its approach to deliver new sensations as well.

I spent dozens of hours playing the World of Warcraft: Forever beta, and I've already put dozens more into the full release. I'm not alone either. Friends are excited about it again and the servers are packed. I've yet to see them prohibitively packed though. Forever uses a new layering system whereby players pick how they want to play the game (Normal, PvP, or Roleplay) and that's it. There are no pesky issues with 'oh no, my friend chose a different server to me and now we can't play' with the game shrewdly keeping an eye on its layers and moving people around as and when that layer gets full to the point it would degrade the play experience.

It mostly works. Early on, friends and I found that we had to manually reset the layer by logging out and back into the game, but after the first day, it worked seamlessly. Such a move is an early example of how Forever is designed to be far more accessible for all.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

Right from the start, you're actually guided through the game with a helpful early tutorial teaching you how to move, attack, and what to do when you die for the first time. As a veteran player, I didn't need it, but I could see the substantial benefit. Retail has a tutorial, but Classic never bothered with one. Another point in favor of this iteration.

Diving in is a beautiful experience once more, especially if you roll one of the new Skyborne race characters. Affectionately referred to as blue Smurfs, these elves are wondrous. They look great amongst a game with improved looking models but none that can rival this new race. You can play either as Alliance or Horde with the Skyborne, with Druid, Hunter, Rogue, and Warrior available for all, while Horde and Alliance have Mage and Shaman classes at their disposal respectively.

There are specific Skyborne racial abilities which are useful too. Walk on Air allows you to glide in the air for 10 seconds which I found a great timesaver when wanting to fall from high distances. There's also Skysight for Horde which boosts run speed by 10%, while Alliance can activate a ley line and gain 100% increased health and mana regeneration for a time. Throughout my time playing WoW, I tend to forget to use my racial abilities – but these are the kind of skills I didn't want to miss out on.

Skyborne also has some delightful new animations such as a unique dance, a cute cheer, and a more appealing jump than most. Druids get new forms as well. It all feels very fresh once more. After extensively leveling with Skyborne, I didn't want to go back to the legacy races. The Skyborne starter zone of Zephras Isle is luscious. It has that typical World of Warcraft feel to it but it's sprawling, colorful, and a gorgeous reflection of how far games have come.

The water effects across the game are more realistic with waves moving naturally, and the lighting reflecting how it should be compared to real life. Even the audio is slightly muffled when you're underwater, although I found I needed good headphones to absorb this fully. There are some great looking weather effects too. Seeing The Barrens on a hot summer's day then the next day encountering bad storms and fog is special.

None of this would matter much if Forever wasn't fun but it is so much fun. Right now, everyone in-game is as welcoming as they were during the original days. I easily found pickup groups but also I'd have random players resurrect me just because they could, or toss me a heal at a key moment. Another time, I was boarding a zeppelin outside of Orgrimmar and the ship was full of shamans in wolf form dancing and encouraging everyone else to join in.

The ship was full of shamans in wolf form dancing and encouraging everyone else to join in.

After years of World of Warcraft feeling lonely and more of a solo MMO, Forever has united players again. At points, I'd be in a silent group, but at others, we'd chat and share our experiences. My Hunter teamed up with a Druid for one quest, and even though neither of us spoke the same language so we communicated through emotes. It was a beautiful moment when we succeeded.

There are new dungeons too covering all level ranges. Hall of Thanes is an early addition for levels 13-18 and it isn't just a matter of 'run and smash' with a bit more strategy easing you into the dungeon mentality, before hitting Ruins of Lordaeron at 15-20, Excavation Site: Wetlands in the 20s, and more beyond that. There's a rich variety here with experience bonuses varying a little while Blizzard balances things. I don't live in dungeons but I've enjoyed diving in to break up the exploration side of the game.

Forever also encourages community spirit with its campfire system. Feeling delightfully RPGish, you can plonk down a campfire and share some buffs with other players. It ties into cooking well, but ultimately it gives you a moment to reflect. While exploring, I've come across plenty of players just camping and chilling. It's a nice reminder that you're playing an MMO, an active world full of players making their own way.

There are still predictable moments of tedium, of course. While Forever has much faster respawns than other versions of the game (sometimes too fast to keep up with in quieter areas), quest item drops can be very hit and miss. At one point, I spent a disproportionate amount of time trying to collect satyr horns from mobs. I needed 16. They only seemed to drop about every 10 mobs despite me being able to see the horns on the satyrs. A boring reminder that sometimes, you'll still be simply grinding away in-game rather than absorbing those beautiful moments of unity.

After 22 years, World of Warcraft: Forever has finally sold me on Blizzard's undying MMO – now I never want to leave

Combat is mostly much how it's always been. There's autoattack via one key press (or button on the action bar) and then you choose other actions such as casting a particular spell, buff, or special attack.

There's now gamepad support which has opened the game up to unofficial Steam Deck support via a few tweaks. I found this worked well enough. Much like Final Fantasy 14's implementation of gamepad support on console, I wouldn't fancy my chances using a gamepad during more intense moments such as dungeons or raids. It's just not quite as precise or as speedy as a traditional mouse and keyboard setup. It is handy for casual play though, and for those times you don't want to be hunched over your keyboard. Ultimately, more choice is only ever a good thing.

Curiously, while Forever has made the game smaller by not implementing expansion packs, it also feels much bigger as exploration is mostly conducted on foot until you can afford a ground mount (no flying mounts here) but it means everything you achieve feels richly earned.

For the most part, Forever is a wondrous success. The game I'm itching to play more of even though it's all I've done for days. There are so many good tweaks that make it better than Classic. A recent ally alert pops up next to players you've recently grouped with, there's Discord integration which was previously only on Retail, and the quest limit is now up to 40 from 20 (incredibly convenient if you're like me and stockpile quest hand-ins).

The promised 1,000 or so new quests are interspersed nicely with many of the early ones linking together zones more cohesively, and introducing professions and other valuable skills well. Some simply provide you with good gear like bags or a weapon that helps you feel strong for a few levels. All the new quests I've experienced feel like they deserved to be there rather than feeling like filler to pad out the experience.

I'm curious about what will happen further down the line for the game. There's a roadmap lined up for new raids in December before we get even more new raids, dungeons, and quests in 2027, so the next 12 months should be pretty exciting. From there, who knows? WoW: Forever focuses on horizontal progression so we won't see a level cap increase but that makes sense for a game that's all about the journey rather than the destination.

As in life, it's a smart move to enjoy the ride rather than think too far ahead and I haven't had this much fun with World of Warcraft in decades. Sometimes that's all you need. Now, if you'll excuse me, I have a Skyborne Hunter seeking my attention. I want to make some new gear for my guild and get raid ready. The good times are finally back.

World of Warcraft: Forever was reviewed on PC, with a code provided by the publisher.

Planning to play forever? Check out our best open world games and best RPGs rankings for more to play now, and keep an eye on our upcoming RPGs calendar for what's on the horizon!

Her main areas of interest include games, B2B, smart tech, wearables, speakers, headphones, and saving you money.

Databricks’ new AI agent tells CEO Ali Ghodsi the unvarnished truth

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Databricks on Wednesday unveiled an updated version of its AI agent Genie, which can now perform multi-step jobs that take extended work to finish. It does this through an ontology that Databricks built to give Genie the context it needs from the information that customers store on the Databricks platform.

Corporate leaders can now assign the agent a wide range of tasks, and it will complete them unsupervised, the company says. To prove the point, Databricks CEO Ali Ghodsi showed me how he uses it internally. Instead of a product demo, he fired up his actual agent and walked me through his most recent chats and charts.

He showed me how it analyzed the parts of an employee satisfaction survey where the company didn’t score well, and how it helped him rate (and discard) fixes for those low scores that some executives suggested. He showed me how it summarized a big sales pitch to a prospect that went poorly — including the features the prospect said it needed before buying.

It also prepped him for a podcast interview by reviewing his previous appearances and developing likely questions. While we talked, he even had it tell him what he should say to me. (And its advice on that wasn’t bad.)

The fascinating part was that Ghodsi was using Genie to find the stuff that he’s now unlikely to hear as the big boss. It was a source of unvarnished truth about what wasn’t going well that he needed to address.

“Have you heard of Dunbar’s number? The magic number is 150,” he said when I pointed this out. Named for British anthropologist Robin Dunbar, it’s theoretically the maximum number of people with whom humans can maintain stable relationships. In a small startup, a CEO knows everyone, has direct relationships with them, and they feel safe to tell him negative info straight up.

With 12,000 employees, Ghodsi is the first to admit he can’t know everyone. At that size, a salesperson isn’t likely to confess a disastrous sales call to the CEO. An HR manager isn’t likely to confess that their idea for increasing employee satisfaction will probably fail.

“Anyone who has more than 150 employees, which is a lot of companies, a lot of people can get value out of this,” he said of AI agents generally and his product, Genie, specifically.

Ghodsi’s own use of Genie (often called dogfooding, from the phrase “eating your own dogfood”) is one reason why Databricks is now pitching it as an always-on agent for business leaders. He says he also uses it in his personal life, rather than a security-questionable alternative like OpenClaw or Instinct or Muse.

He believes Genie can do for the business side what AI, agents and harnesses (the software that wraps around an AI model and lets it use tools) are doing for programmers. As for the risk that agents will ratchet up corporate AI costs, Ghodsi has an answer for that, too. He said costs can be controlled through Databricks’ Unity Gateway product. It allows companies to set usage limits and to swap in less expensive open-weight models.

Ocarina of Time is even better if you use a smartphone app

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I never liked hunting for Golden Skulltulas in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. The game has 100 of them tucked all over Hyrule, and even over my many hours with the N64 classic growing up, I can’t remember ever actually finding all of them, or even wanting to. Jumping into the Ocarina of Time remake, I expected to ignore the Golden Skulltulas once again. But while poking around Nintendo’s Zelda Notes companion app, I discovered that its maps are a huge help to tracking down all of the elusive golden spiders — and pretty much every other collectible in the game — and now I find myself constantly opening up the app to make sure I haven’t missed a secret.

Inside Zelda Notes, which is available in the free Nintendo Switch app (you don’t need a Switch Online subscription to use it), you can peruse a big map of Hyrule and tap on individual areas for a closer look. The app offers just enough direction to nudge me toward an elusive pickup; when zoomed in, the app drops icons on the map showing you the locations of different collectibles, and if you tap on an icon, the app will show a popup noting any items or special requirements you need to fulfill to be able to obtain it. There are also a bunch of filters you can apply, like swapping between the maps for child and adult Link and toggling categories of icons on and off.

For Skulltula hunting, the map has already saved my sanity many times. While exploring, I’ll often come across the telltale Golden Skulltula sound but have no idea how to get to the tricky spider, even with the help of additional chimes from the new Echo Gem Skulltula tracking item. But with a peek at the Zelda Notes app, I can pinpoint the exact location of where the Skulltula is supposed to be and if I need to use a certain item or change the time of day to be able to get to it.

I recently delved into the Shadow Temple, for example, and I couldn’t figure out how to get up to a Skulltula that I saw while coming down an elevator. But when I checked the icon on the Zelda Notes map, it said that I needed the Scarecrow Song to create a hookshot point, which I hadn’t gotten yet. As soon as I finished the dungeon, getting the Scarecrow Song was my first priority.

Zelda Notes has been useful for tracking down Pieces of Heart, too, which give Link more health once you collect enough. While running up and down Zora’s River, I kept seeing two Pieces of Heart on faraway ledges that I couldn’t figure out how to get to. (My memories of my extensive time with Ocarina of Time as a kid also failed me.) But tapping one of the icons on the Zelda Notes map revealed that I needed a Cucco to get to it — and suddenly, I understood why there was one of the chirping white birds near the entrance of the zone.

Yes, it can be annoying to have to open my phone and dig through an app to track down all of the Skulltulas and other objects of curiosity scattered around Hyrule. I play video games to try and get away from my phone, not look at it more. And sure, I could just look up an online guide, but that often means wading through walkthroughs of varying quality, trusting an AI summary, or scrubbing through a YouTube video. But with Zelda Notes, I’m usually in and out relatively quickly, putting down my phone after seeing a clue or an icon that pushes me in the direction of the next golden spider. I’ve already found more than 70 Golden Skulltulas, and to my surprise, I now want to hunt for the full 100.

Google’s AlphaGenome 2 zooms in on the cellular roots of disease

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

DeepMind’s new DNA model better predicts how mutations affect different cells, potentially opening paths to treatment.

DeepMind’s new DNA model better predicts how mutations affect different cells, potentially opening paths to treatment.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Researchers at Google DeepMind have unveiled a new AI model designed to unravel how changes in our DNA affect different cells, giving scientists a more detailed view of a notoriously difficult problem that could hold important clues to the causes of disease — and point the way to new treatments.

AlphaGenome 2 is the successor to DeepMind’s genome-predicting AI model AlphaGenome, which was released last year. While nearly every cell in the human body carries the same DNA, different types of cells — from heart muscle cells and skin cells to immune cells fighting an infection — switch different genes on and off, and those patterns can change depending on what a cell is doing. As a result, the same genetic variant can have very different effects depending on the type and state of the cell.

The new model is designed to capture those differences, predicting the effects of genetic variants on individual cell types and states. Google said in a blog post published Wednesday that it offers a much finer-grained view than broader models like AlphaGenome, potentially revealing disease-relevant effects in specific cells that might otherwise go unnoticed.

AlphaGenome 2 was trained on more than 100 million individual cell profiles across 68 tissues to give it that more detailed view. DeepMind says the model effectively turns static cell atlases — huge data repositories that map and catalog individual cells in detail — “into a predictive engine for human genetics.” According to the company, AlphaGenome 2 identifies nearly twice as many variants that can change gene expression in cells than its previous state-of-the-art model.

Notably, the researchers found that AlphaGenome 2 could also make predictions about new types of cells and biological states without needing to be retrained. It could also identify gaps in experimental data, potentially helping scientists decide what to investigate next in the lab.

For Adam Gayoso, a research scientist at Google DeepMind involved in the project, the big breakthrough is “tying human genetics to cell biology.” AlphaGenome 2 gives researchers a way to drill down on genetic variants identified in large populations, to see what effects they may be having on a cellular level, and potentially connect those effects to disease, he told The Verge.

Google DeepMind’s genomics lead Žiga Avsec told The Verge that this specificity is important if you have a goal like figuring out how to treat a particular disease. “When you develop drugs, you’re targeting specific proteins and specific cell types,” he explained, which makes having this kind of granularity vital.

Building a model like AlphaGenome 2 has only recently become possible thanks to new technologies that can capture individual cells and their states at high resolution and create the vast datasets needed to train it, Avsec said. DeepMind is now working to improve its genetics models by generating more data, including for cell states that aren’t represented well today. Avsec said future versions could also potentially be trained on data “across multiple species,” giving the models a broader view of biology. Longer term, he said models like AlphaGenome 2 could feed into efforts to create “virtual cells,” digital simulations designed to mimic biological systems.

Although Avsec said he views AlphaGenome 2 as a successor to AlphaGenome, it isn’t replacing it any time soon. He said its more detailed predictions come at a computational cost and are “more expensive” to produce than those of its predecessor, he said. Those demands would have made it impractical to use AlphaGenome 2 to produce the genome-wide Atlas covering “every possible DNA letter change in the human genome” that DeepMind released in September.

Google says it is making AlphaGenome 2 available for non-commercial research via the AlphaGenome API from today, with a limited “private preview” for commercial research.

The model is the latest in a growing array of efforts by Google and rival AI labs to use the technology to tackle major problems in science and medicine. Pushmeet Kohli, who heads DeepMind’s AI for science research, has hinted that more impressive scientific results are on the way, and the organization is expected to open an automated materials science lab in the UK soon. Anthropic, meanwhile, has been experimenting with drug design and given Claude a wet lab, while OpenAI has been mowing down an impressive amount of longstanding problems in mathematics and related fields.
